#7417. The changing CMO role in 3 numbers

Let Marketing Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The AI skill gap, technological disconnect and other stats surrounding the current state of marketing’s top dogs.

Artificial intelligence, shifting C-suite dynamics and an increased focus on short-term growth have the potential to fundamentally alter what being a chief marketing officer means. And, all of this comes as marketing budgets continue to hover around 8% of company revenue, meaning marketers are having to do more with less.

The actual title of CMO may also be becoming rarer. Nearly one-third of S&P 500 companies have no CMO at all, similar to what it’s been in the past. However, more companies are creating roles that combine marketing and other business functions, such as sales.

As the industry continues to figure out what the modern CMO looks like, Marketing Dive has compiled some numbers which demonstrate how the CMO role is changing and what that could mean for the future.

The percentage of CMOs hired this year who have AI-related skills, compared to 9% of CMOs who have had the role for 4-plus years.

The demand for AI skills among marketing heads has increased significantly, reflecting the mounting expectations CMOs face to take charge of the technology. This shift didn’t come from nowhere. The majority of marketers believe that AI is going to change the industry, yet just 32% believe they need to significantly update their skills, per Gartner data.

With CMO tenure lengths falling 35% since 2010,  personal skill development should be a priority, according to a study by Findem and CMO Huddles. While one-third of CMOs have held the position for over five years, 30% have been in the role for less than 18 months. The study also found that newly appointed CMOs tend to have a wider skill set, including in areas such as AI and demand generation.

The pressure to adapt is significant. The industry has seen the rise of the “CMO-plus” with the increased popularity of roles such as chief commercial officer, which oversee marketing operations and sales, per Spencer Stuart.

The percentage of CMOs who say their organizations have an “excellent” ability to adopt and innovate with new marketing technologies.

While CMOs face increased pressure to be a steward of AI for their organizations, only 7% of CMOs rate their organization’s ability to adopt and innovate with new marketing technologies as “poor,” according to the “CMO Outlook 2026” report from Lippincott and Bloomberg Media. Marketing heads cite challenges in accessing or integrating company-wide data, limited speed and capacity for experimentation as major hurdles to technology enablement, per the report.

CMOs see marketing as ahead of the game, with 65% saying marketing’s adoption of new technology is significantly better or better than the rest of the company. However, a focus on short-term gains and optimization may be coming at a cost. Ethical and security concerns surrounding AI all ranked as low priorities for marketing executives, according to the report. As consumers continue to express dissatisfaction with AI-generated campaigns, marketing heads could be sacrificing short-term gains in C-suite influence for long-term brand stability.

Yet, CMOs are incentivized to continue prioritizing short-term gains. While tenure for the position remains short, 65% of those who left the role either found a similar or better position elsewhere or were promoted internally, according to Spencer Stuart data. Ten percent moved onto the CEO position.

The percentage of senior marketing leaders who plan to increase investments in AI assistants in the next year.

Despite only 23% of consumers saying they trust AI recommendations, the majority of senior marketing leaders plan on increasing investments in AI assistants, per Kantar research. The increased reliance on the technology comes as marketers are expected to do more with less. Marketing budgets have remained relatively flat, and 56% of CMOs do not think their organizations have the budgets to execute their 2026 strategy, according to Gartner.

However, the increased reliance on AI could prove problematic down the line. Nearly 20% of marketers have eliminated some functional roles because of the technology and Gartner predicts that many marketing firms will be able to eliminate traditional entry-level jobs by 2030. This could potentially lead to a talent shortage in the future and CMOs may have to work harder to nurture new talent or the industry as a whole could suffer.

How much AI is actually relieving pain points for marketers remains a debated topic. AI claims a significant percentage of marketing budgets, an average of 15.3% to 21.3%, depending on the organization. The technology meant to cut costs has proven expensive while its impact on business outcomes remains unclear.

Then there is the potential cost associated with losing the human touch. Sixty-two percent of marketers have used the technology either moderately or extensively for market research, which could potentially lead to a lot of similar looking content. More broadly, overreliance can potentially lead to a lack of critical-thinking skills among team members.

#7416. How To Run A Full-Funnel Ecommerce Account Through BFCM Week via @sejournal, @tonyadam

See how Search Engine Journal checks a real site for crawler access and ranks fixes by business impact.

Invalid traffic on AI Max search doubled to 5.28%. Get the channel-by-channel retail benchmarks before Black Friday.

See how Search Engine Journal checks a real site for crawler access and ranks fixes by business impact.

Explore Ahrefs’ Q2, Q3 2026 AI search benchmarks and practical next steps.

Ask the SEJ experts you already read what AI, agents, and algorithm changes mean for your traffic. Private, human to human. $97/mo.

AI Search Tracking Checklist: How to Attribute Calls From ChatGPT, Claude, and Perplexity

Build demand before BFCM with Meta prospecting, give Google budget room for peak sales, and retarget holiday visitors to drive December purchases.

This year, Black Friday is Nov. 27 and Cyber Monday is Nov. 30 and winning Black Friday/Cyber Monday (BFCM) week with a full-funnel ecommerce account comes down to addressing three things, done in order. Fill the funnel in early November so there is more demand to capture, give your bidding room to capture it efficiently during the week, and keep selling to that same audience through December.

In September, I covered how to structure a full-funnel ecommerce account before Q4, meaning the exclusions, customer lists, and prospecting budget. This article is the playbook for running that account through peak season, phase by phase.

In my experience, the BFCM weeks that go best in our accounts are the ones where the work started in early November. By Black Friday, every retailer in your category is bidding for the same shoppers. In our accounts, what converts during Q4, specifically BFCM and Holiday, comes from people who found you in the weeks, or even months before. We see shoppers holding out for your best offers during this time and it’s your job to remind them they were looking, waiting to purchase, and get them to purchase. Your job leading up to the holidays is building up your prospecting and growing your funnel as much as possible to expand that audience that would convert down funnel during BFCM.

The last thing you want heading into BFCM is your Meta ad sets dropping back into the learning phase. An ad set usually exits learning after about 50 results in the week after its last significant edit, and Meta notes that ad sets in learning are less stable and usually have a higher CPA. Meta also warns that frequent budget changes can send an ad set back into learning. So step budgets up gradually through early November, and your prospecting will be stable and spending efficiently before peak week instead of relearning the week it matters most. You don’t want to overpay during a season where you’re already paying above your baseline.

Meta recommends waiting to edit until an ad set is out of learning, because editing an ad, ad set, or campaign during the learning phase resets it. Build your BFCM creative early and launch it in the days (or week(s), depending on learning) before Black Friday, so the learning happens ahead of peak traffic instead of during it. On Google, the retailer guidance is to upload new holiday asset groups at least two weeks ahead so they are approved in time, and asset group scheduling lets you switch them on when the sale starts.

Google’s retailer guidance notes that a lower ROAS target can help maximize visibility for priority products in the weeks leading up to peak periods, when many people are browsing. So if you plan on lowering ROAS targets to build volume, do it in early November, before Black Friday week. Keep track of where your targets and budgets started so you know exactly what to reset them to in December, and while you’re at it, decide which date ranges you’ll compare in your January review.

By now, you should have already decided the offer so everything downstream can be built around it. Merchant Center promotions let you mark an offer for new customers, and Google shows it only when it judges a shopper likely to be new. That puts your best offer in front of the people your prospecting is bringing in, but it does not guarantee that existing buyers won’t see it. Here is a quick checklist for Merchant Center we use:

Once the week starts, the goal is capturing as much of the demand you built leading up to BFCM as you can, at the revenue you need and a ROAS you are happy with.

Google’s retailer guidance is to plan budgets early and stay ready to capture extra sales, and its budget recommendations will flag campaigns where your ads could stop running on your busiest days. A campaign can spend up to two times your daily budget but no more than 30.4 times your average daily budget cap in a month. So you just want to be careful not to overspend leading into Black Friday and run out of room to spend during your peak revenue time of the year.

Smart Bidding already accounts for seasonal events like the holidays, but Google built seasonality adjustments so you can tell it about expected conversion rate changes from a specific promotion or sale ahead of time. Google’s retail guidance is to use one for short events where you expect conversion rates to change by more than 30% in under seven days, like Black Friday weekend, and only on campaigns with conversion history. You can set this from past results and if Black Friday converted at roughly double your November baseline yearly, on average, that is your input. For a longer promotion or smaller conversion rate fluctuations, adjust your ROAS targets manually instead, and remember to set them back afterward. A seasonality adjustment, by contrast, returns bidding to normal on its own when the window ends.

During BFCM week, in our accounts, prospecting CPMs hit their peak and prospecting doesn’t look great on reporting, because branded search and retargeting are converting at their cheapest. I would continue Top of Funnel (TOFU) prospecting because people are still in discovery mode but those mid-to-bottom funnel harvesting campaigns are going to be where the additional budget should be spent. On our accounts selling higher consideration products, we regularly see shoppers who first came in through November prospecting buy in December, which is why we keep it running through BFCM week.

Work with the team to confirm nothing is broken going into BFCM and what should be acted on during that week:

Higher Meta CPMs, a single bad day of ROAS, Performance Max shifting spend between asset groups, and higher retargeting frequency are all normal for peak week in our accounts. They can wait until December 2.

Early December is where the prospecting campaigns you kept running pay off. In our accounts it usually comes with less competition than Black Friday itself, which is great for ROAS.

Everyone who came to your site during peak week and left without purchasing is now your warmest audience. Lean into that. Build retargeting around them on both platforms, and lead with shipping deadlines rather than a deeper discount. In our accounts, the urgency of getting an order in before the shipping cutoff in the second week of December is one of the strongest messages of the season. It also lets you keep selling without training shoppers to wait for the next wave of discounts.

When promo pricing comes off, conversion rates fall back toward normal. In our accounts that usually shows up as a few days of overbidding in early December that settles on its own. Reset targets back to where they were before the holidays, and give it a few days before reacting to the overbidding. Then clean up. Let sale price dates expire, end finished promotions, and pull countdown copy and time-limited assets from every campaign, including the ones nobody remembers building.

The new customers coming through in early December are the first real read on whether the full-funnel work paid off, and they are the number to bring into your January review.

Winning with a full-funnel ecommerce account through BFCM week is mostly decided before Thanksgiving. Ramp Meta prospecting in steps, launch creative, and move Google targets early, and lock your offer and Merchant Center setup. Then give the bidder headroom and one seasonality adjustment once the week starts, hold prospecting flat, and spend early December converting the audience you built.

If you only do one thing this week, pull last year’s Black Friday conversion rate against your November baseline. It tells you whether a seasonality adjustment is worth setting and exactly what to set it to.

Featured Image: Roman Samborskyi/Shutterstock

Early November at the latest. Meta ad sets need about 50 results after a significant edit to exit learning, and Google recommends uploading new holiday asset groups at least two weeks ahead so they are approved in time, so budget ramps and creative both need lead time before Black Friday.

Usually not. In our accounts, prospecting looks expensive on reporting during peak because branded search and retargeting are converting at their cheapest, but people are still in discovery mode, and the audience prospecting builds in late November is a big part of what buys in December.

Yes, if you expect your conversion rate to change by more than about 30% over less than a week and the campaign has conversion history. For a longer promotion or a smaller lift, Google recommends adjusting ROAS or CPA targets manually instead. Base the adjustment on your own past promo results rather than a round number.

Enough that your best campaigns are not capped on the busiest days. A campaign can spend up to twice its average daily budget on a given day but no more than 30.4 times it across the month, so a budget sized for an average November day can run out on Black Friday. Google’s budget recommendations will also flag campaigns at risk of not running on your busiest days.

Retarget BFCM week visitors who did not buy, and lead with shipping deadlines rather than a deeper discount. Reset bidding targets back to where they were before the holidays, then let sale price dates expire, end finished promotions and pull countdown copy from every campaign.

#7415. Amazon’s ad revenue growth decelerates due to Prime Day tweaks

Let Marketing Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Pushing the sales event to June and a lack of NBA content led to slower ad revenue growth in Q3, but AI agents aren’t disrupting the segment just yet.

Amazon’s revenue derived from advertising grew 12% year over year to $19.6 billion in Q3 as changes to the timing of key sales events and streaming programs affected performance, according to an earnings statement. Those results mark a noteworthy deceleration in the rate of growth from the year-ago period, when Amazon’s ad segment was up 22%, as well as Q2 2026, which delivered ads revenue growth of 26%. Amazon’s stock tanked following the report, which missed analyst expectations on several key fronts, including advertising.

The e-commerce giant in the release attributed the ad shortfall in Q3 to “timing anomalies” stemming from Prime Day. Amazon moved the dayslong deals bonanza from July to June for the first time in several years, which shifted when the windfall from Prime Day landed. Scheduling of the NBA season on Prime Video — Q2 and Q4 both have pro basketball programming, while Q3 does not — further slowed momentum. Amazon in Q3 also lapped a point in time last year when it overhauled its search marketing programs in a way that helped monetize traffic at a higher rate, executives explained.

One area not worrying Amazon quite yet is the impact of artificial intelligence agents that can disintermediate the role advertising plays on the shopping journey. Consumer-facing personal agents that can hunt for deals and shop on the users’ behalf, including Meta’s Muse, have quickly become popular. Amazon blocked Muse from accessing its e-commerce marketplace last month.

“A natural question is whether agentic shopping from external horizontal agents is meaningfully impacting Amazon Ads, and the answer is no,” Amazon CEO Andy Jassy said on a call discussing the Q3 earnings with investors. “Few customers are doing much shopping this way yet, as the experience is still not great nor more convenient than coming to a retailer directly to research or one-click order there.”

Amazon is advancing its own bets in the agentic space, including an Ads Agent that helps marketers build, optimize and run campaigns based on parameters like budget and duration. Ads Agent in September introduced full-funnel capabilities, meaning it can unify sponsored ads, display, video and streaming TV in one effort.

Amazon’s Q3 earnings echo that of other digital ad platforms. Google also posted a slowdown for its core search and YouTube services amid a transition to AI products that are slower to monetize. The tech category generally is coming under greater pressure to justify steep spending on AI. Amazon’s capital expenditures reached $75.9 billion in Q3 while negative free cash flow hit $42.4 billion.

Amazon forecasts ad revenue growth to be in the high teens in Q4, a critical period that includes the holidays. That will be another slowdown compared to the same period last year, when ads grew 22%.

#7413. SDK Engineer - Python at Supabase

Supabase is the Postgres development platform, built by developers for developers. We provide a complete backend solution including Database, Auth, Storage, Edge Functions, Realtime, and Vector Search. All services are deeply integrated and designed for growth.

We're looking for a SDK Engineer - Python to join our SDK team and own how developers talk to Supabase from Python. You'll work closely with the rest of the SDK team, our Auth, Realtime, Storage, PostgREST, Functions, CLI and Docs teams, and the open-source community, helping us make the Python SDK a library Python developers genuinely enjoy using.

Python developers reach Supabase from backend services, data pipelines, notebooks, and a growing number of AI agents and agent frameworks. The Python SDK is how all of them talk to the platform: database queries, auth, storage, realtime and edge functions, in both sync and async flavors.

This is a library-authoring role, and the work happens in the open: public repos, public issue trackers, and API decisions whose consequences last for years. If you get satisfaction from a type checker that catches the bug before your users do, from an async client that cleans up after itself, and from an issue tracker that isn't a graveyard, you'll like it here.

This role is ideal for someone who thrives in async, fast-paced environments and is excited about building developer tools that scale to millions.

Build and evolve our Python SDK across database, auth, storage, realtime and functions, with both sync and async APIs that feel natural in Python.

Start new things. Alongside the maintenance work, find what's missing, make the case for it, and build it. Nobody will hand you the list.

Work in the open. Triage inbound issues, review and shepherd outside contributions, keep CI and release automation healthy, and hold a clear, kind line on scope.

Own the typing experience. Make the SDK fully typed and checker-friendly, so autocomplete and static analysis stay correct in real codebases.

Keep us honest across environments. Multiple Python versions, sync and async frameworks, workers, serverless and notebooks — with dependency ranges that don't fight our users' projects. Test it, don't assume it.

Work on realtime. Long-lived websocket connections, reconnection, and subscription and presence semantics.

Track the platform. When auth, realtime, storage, postgrest or edge functions ship a change, make sure the SDK reflects it, and that we say so in the changelog.

Design in public. Write the RFCs, migration guides, release notes and docs. Treat a breaking change as a deprecation path plus a migration story, not a version bump.

Make our SDK legible to AI coding agents, which are now a major consumer of it.

Know modern Python typing deeply. You use generics, protocols, overloads and TypedDict as design tools, run strict type checking, and fix type errors without reaching for Any.

Have authored or maintained a published library with a public API and users outside your own company. You know what it costs to change something people depend on.

Know the maintainer side of open source. Issue triage at volume, reviewing outside PRs, release automation, changelogs and semver, security reports, and saying no to a feature request without losing the person who asked.

Are fluent in asyncio and have opinions on how a library should serve both sync and async callers.

Know Python packaging for libraries: pyproject.toml, wheels, modern tooling, version support policy, and why libraries shouldn't pin their dependencies tightly.

Test as a habit: unit, type-level and end-to-end against a real stack. Every fix comes with a regression test.

Write well. Design docs, changelogs and issue replies are a large part of the output here, much of it public, and we're async by default.

Have real autonomy: you find the important work, scope it, ship it, and say what you didn't do.

Communicate clearly across both technical and non-technical audiences

Have experience in async or globally distributed teams

Are energized by solving real-world problems for developers

Are comfortable navigating ambiguity and moving quickly

Bonus points if you: have run a major-version migration for a public package, know your way around JWT/OAuth, have built integrations for AI agent frameworks, or have generated idiomatic clients from a schema.

We hire globally. We believe you can do your best work from anywhere. There are no Supabase offices, but we provide a WeWork membership or co-working allowance you can use anywhere in the world.

Every team member receives ESOP (equity ownership) in the company. We want everyone to share in the upside of what we’re building together.

Use this budget to set up your ideal work environment—laptop, monitor, headphones, or whatever helps you do your best work.

Supabase covers 100% of health insurance for employees and 80% for dependents, wherever you are. Your wellbeing and your family’s health are important to us.

Once a year, the entire company gathers in a new city for a week of connection, collaboration, and fun. It’s a highlight of our year.

We operate asynchronously and trust you to manage your own time. You know what needs to be done and when.

Every team member receives an annual education allowance to spend on learning—courses, books, conferences, or anything that supports your growth.

Supabase was born-remote and open-source-first. We believe our globally distributed team is our secret weapon in building tools developers love.

Over $1B raised (including our $500M Series F)

We move fast, build in public, and use what we ship. If it’s in your project, we probably use it in ours too. We believe deeply in the open-source ecosystem and strive to support—not replace—existing tools and communities.

New remote jobs delivered to your inbox, every week.

Location independence is the future of work. It serves companies with best talent from around the globe. And benefits employees with a greater work life balance and the ability to work in their pyjamas.- Nithur, founder.

#7412. Google Says No AI Click Reporting News Yet, Ahead Of UK Deadline via @sejournal, @MattGSouthern

See how Search Engine Journal checks a real site for crawler access and ranks fixes by business impact.

Invalid traffic on AI Max search doubled to 5.28%. Get the channel-by-channel retail benchmarks before Black Friday.

See how Search Engine Journal checks a real site for crawler access and ranks fixes by business impact.

Explore Ahrefs’ Q2, Q3 2026 AI search benchmarks and practical next steps.

Ask the SEJ experts you already read what AI, agents, and algorithm changes mean for your traffic. Private, human to human. $97/mo.

AI Search Tracking Checklist: How to Attribute Calls From ChatGPT, Claude, and Perplexity

Google says it has nothing to announce on adding more AI search data to Search Console, ahead of a UK compliance date.

According to Gary Illyes of Google, the team is “super interested in giving more data,” but the search company has nothing to announce yet about more Search Console data. In response to a LinkedIn post referencing anonymous comments by Google staff regarding the potential arrival of click and query data for AI Mode and AI Overviews, Illyes stated that he didn’t think Google had phrased it like that.

The Search Console Generative AI Performance Report counts impressions for both AI Mode and AI Overviews collectively. The clicks from each of these features are included within the standard Performance report as part of all web results.

In the UK, the Competition and Markets Authority (CMA) lists clicks and click-through rate among the AI search metrics Google must provide, in a summary of its publisher rules. The CMA gives Google until Dec. 3 to comply.

In his LinkedIn post, SEO professional Rajeev Lekhwar attributes a quote about Search Console click and query data to unnamed Google staff. He tags several Googlers, including Illyes and John Mueller, but doesn’t identify the speaker. The quote reads:

“The clicks and queries data is the most requested data set by website owners and SEOs. We are also anticipating this data to be available in Google Search Console but, even we don’t know by when this data will be added”.

Illyes replied in the comments early Monday:

“I don’t think we’ve phrased it like this. The team is super interested in giving more data, data that makes sense, but we don’t have anything to announce yet.”

Lekhwar’s post includes the hashtags #searchcentrallive2026 and #sclblr2026, which point to Google’s Search Central Live event in Bengaluru on Oct. 30. According to Google’s announcement, the event was in person only, with no live stream or recording available.

In a separate Bluesky thread about Illyes’ comment, Mueller wrote that there’s “lots of helpful data which isn’t currently in Search Console” and that what counts as helpful data changes over time.

The Generative AI Report provided in Search Console counts impressions (each time AI Overviews or AI Mode shows links to your site) and provides a breakdown of those impressions by page, country, device, and date according to its help page. However, the page doesn’t list clicks, CTR, or queries. As I previously reported, Google announced the roll-out of this report to all sites globally on Aug. 31.

AI clicks are included in the standard Performance report. According to Google’s help page regarding how clicks are counted, when you click on a link within an AI Overview or AI Mode it will be considered one click; additionally if you ask another question using AI Mode it is considered a new query. The standard performance report lists Search Appearance Types, and none of them covers AI Overviews or AI Mode.

Google will have to provide publishers with “clear and detailed metrics on user engagement” (the CMA’s summary lists “impressions, clicks, and click-through rate, aggregated across features such as AI Overviews and AI Mode”) with their content when using the company’s AI search functions; this is according to the CMA’s rules.

According to the CMA’s interpretive notes, these metrics should also include a breakdown of all data by each individual publisher, as well as being able to distinguish whether or not a publisher’s content was shown in an AI feature versus in an organic search result. This information should be provided via “a commonly accessible platform, such as Google Search Console.” Google can meet the rules another way if it shows the CMA it complies, and they cover AI features shown to UK users.

You can see how frequently your pages show up in AI Overviews and AI Mode combined, however the click-throughs from these will be muddled within your web search totals and there is no way through the performance report to segregate this out using the dimensions as defined by Google. Illyes does not provide an estimated time frame for when this data would start being reported, so for now AI search reporting will need to be built around impressions.

Under the CMA’s rules, page-level controls for AI search features are due three months after the Dec. 3 compliance date. Google’s first compliance report to the CMA covers June 3 through Dec. 3 and is due Jan. 3, 2027. The CMA says it expects to issue a separate notice requiring Google to publish a summary or non-confidential version of each report.

#7411. Google says AI chatbots are hurting its bottom line

The company may be losing its stronghold on discovery as more users turn to tools such as ChatGPT and Claude for answers.

Google said that the rising use of artificial intelligence chatbots, including its own AI answers, has impacted its core ad business, and could become an even bigger revenue impediment moving forward.

As part of parent company Alphabet’s Q3 earnings update last week, Google reported that search volume grew by 12% in Q3. That’s a much slower growth rate than Q2’s 17% growth or Q1’s 19% growth. The report also showed that the company’s search revenue was flat in Q3.

Google CEO Sundar Pichai initially said that the company’s evolving AI Mode search saw strong momentum and its AI overviews drive improved discovery and searcher engagement.

But later in the company’s earnings call, Google SVP Anat Ashkenazi said that while AI Mode usage is rising, the growth of these discovery options is having an impact on its ad business.

As per Ashkenazi: “It's worth noting that the strong growth in AI Mode queries had an impact because of the lower monetization we were seeing in AI Mode. To be clear, we feel very good about our trajectory here. Our ad experiments have shown promising results and we're now scaling ads within both AI Overviews and AI Mode to more users and more queries; including, as I mentioned, international markets and multiturn ads in the U.S.”

Google’s search results are declining over time. As more people use AI chatbots like ChatGPT and Meta’s Muse Google’s discovery usage could be impacted, even though the company has long held an unassailable lead in search.

As Ashkenazi said, Google is planning to resolve this by increasing ad displays in its AI products, which will help to offset some of those losses. But as more people rely on conversational answers for their queries, that shrinks the opportunity for Google to display query-relevant ads, which is the core driver of its ad business.

Essentially, Google is harming its own revenue potential by implementing AI answers. Yet, at the same time, it’s possible that the broader industry shift towards AI chatbots would have forced Google’s hand on this front either way.

It’s unclear whether Google should continue to pump out AI chatbot responses to queries, especially if that’s only going to end up costing the company more money.

It seems like a losing equation, as AI development costs rise and search ad revenue declines. This also highlights a broader mismatch around AI usage in general because the cost-to-benefit ratio may not match up.

In addition, up to 10% of Google’s AI overviews provide incorrect information, according to a report from The New York Times in July. Also,many others provide flawed or incorrect links, so it begs the question as to whether these AI responses provide real value.

This could be a key note in the rising skepticism around AI tools more broadly.

#7410. Why JCPenney is taking on deal fatigue for its holiday campaign

Let Marketing Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Marisa Thalberg, chief customer and marketing officer at parent Catalyst Brands, explains the retailer’s focus on real people and no-fuss ways to save.

JCPenney is attempting to alleviate the deal fatigue felt by consumers headed into the holiday season with a new campaign focused on value and no-fuss ways to save, according to details shared with Marketing Dive.

The retailer’s holiday campaign launches Monday across TV, online video, audio and paid social and spotlights real deal-scoring JCPenney shoppers. The brand will also help consumers navigate the season with a weekly deals cheat sheet, in-store activations and the return of its “Really Big Deals” promotions.

A 15-second spot, titled “Party Fits,” is set in Crown Point, Indiana, and stars a local barber named Raemonie, who showcases three holiday looks that she purchased for a total of $168. In the ad, a voice-over says, “Meet Raemonie — she was worried about having enough outfits for all of her holiday parties. Now, thanks to JCPenney, she’s worried about having enough holiday parties for all her outfits.”

The campaign, which builds on a “Yes, JCPenney” brand platform launched in 2025, was created with agency Mischief and follows a back-to-school effort that tapped real teachers for the creative.

“While everyone else is doing kind of their fantastical TV campaigns for holiday, ours is truly grounded in real people, real life, and showing how those real people can — and deserve to — enjoy having better deals from JCPenney,” said Marisa Thalberg, executive vice president and chief customer and marketing officer at parent Catalyst Brands.

JCPenney’s latest campaign taps into the insight that consumers are overwhelmed with deals as brands attempt to break through amid a period marked by economic uncertainty. A barrage of deals may also be leaving consumers feeling skeptical about whether or not they are actually saving any money at all. Only 32% of shoppers report that they completely or mostly trust retailers to offer a fair or competitive price, according to Akeneo data cited in press details.

On the flip side, 92% of consumers say they are confident that they receive good deals at JCPenney, per information shared by the brand. To help consumers navigate its various promotions, JCPenney will release a cheat sheet each Monday throughout the season highlighting the top deals to shop each week. The “Really Big Deals” promotional offer will run every Thursday through Dec. 13.

From Nov. 5-11, 97% of JCPenney’s in-store items will be on sale. Additionally, the retailer is offering Black Friday deals from Nov. 20-29, and surprise giveaways in stores on Nov. 27 at various points throughout the day.

In hopes of attracting additional foot traffic, JCPenney teamed with Warner Bros. Global Consumer Products around Harry Potter for pop-up events and to bring a version of the Hogwarts Express train to select stores. A limited-time Harry Potter capsule offers various clothing and home items.

Consumers are expected to spend an average of $1,654 this season, a 4% increase over the year prior, as they anticipate higher prices, according to Deloitte’s annual holiday survey. While 38% percent of shoppers plan to adopt five or more money-saving strategies, that share of consumers is also expected to spend 12% more than others on average.

The holiday season will be critical for JCPenney amid ongoing sales struggles. The company saw total net sales fall over 8% year over year to $1.3 billion in the second quarter, a weaker performance than others in the department store segment, including Kohl’s and Macy’s.

Deals have been a staple of JCPenney’s marketing, both around the holidays and beyond. Earlier this year, the retailer remade Paris Fashion Week with a runway show that swapped haute couture for JCPenney looks, with prices displayed on stage. In August, the brand parodied off-price retailers as a way to tout the quality it offers despite its low prices.

With its latest move, JCPenney hopes to continue reinforcing for its shoppers that they don’t have to choose between fashionable items and affordability, Thalberg explained.

“We understand our customer,” Thalberg said. “Our customer is everyone, real people, real working middle class American families who are trying to make ends meet, and we are very cognizant and empathetic of the economic pressures that Americans are facing.”

#7409. How The Athletic Gets More Clicks With One Simple Trick

Spend less time on publishing and reports, and more on helpful content. November 18, 2 PM ET.

Invalid traffic on AI Max search doubled to 5.28%. Get the channel-by-channel retail benchmarks before Black Friday.

See how Search Engine Journal checks a real site for crawler access and ranks fixes by business impact.

Explore Ahrefs’ Q2, Q3 2026 AI search benchmarks and practical next steps.

Ask the SEJ experts you already read what AI, agents, and algorithm changes mean for your traffic. Private, human to human. $97/mo.

The Athletic uses comment counts to highlight its most discussion-worthy content. It's something more publishers should experiment with.

Getting people to click on articles is becoming increasingly difficult. We’ve been lazily conditioned by big tech’s greed, and it shows no sign of slowing.

To combat this, we have to build and own audiences and get better at marketing. Publishers need to work harder to make their content appealing, particularly on the surfaces they control.

The Athletic does something very simple on its homepage that more publishers should consider: It shows readers how many comments selected articles have generated.

It tells you which content its readers really care about.

Now part of the world’s most successful publisher, The Athletic has gone from strength to strength in recent times. In Q2 of 2025, revenue was up one-third to $54 million with an adjusted operating profit of $5.8 million. Hell, even ad revenue nearly doubled to $14.1 million.

Safe to say The New York Times, and by extension The Athletic, are publishing’s most profitable case study. They are worth listening to.

It tells you, dear apathetic reader, which articles are generating discussion. It’s an extremely simple, clever use of social proof.

Depending on the importance you place on comments and engagement, this can be foundational to your on-site distribution strategy. (Image Credit: Harry Clarkson-Bennett)

People are naturally drawn to things other people are interested in. And every publisher is trying to create a community. These comment counts signal which articles are generating discussion among its audience. Arguably other like-minded people.

If one article has 500 comments and another has just one, all things being equal, I’m far more likely to click on the former. It has piqued my curiosity. Clickability goes beyond just images and headlines.

It tells me there’s a conversation worth joining. Or, at the very least, something interesting enough to get people talking.

This is particularly valuable if you have a large homepage and direct audience. As traffic continues to drop, you’ve got to make more use of what you have.

Comments are just one measure of engagement. We can be far more rigorous.

Experiment with shares, likes, and other signals to identify content that resonates with your audience. Tell your audience, with certainty, which articles are worth reading.

Not every article will generate hundreds of comments, even the more popular ones. Some might be widely shared, others might encourage unusually high levels of engagement relative to your traffic.

The important thing is identifying what your audience values and making that visible. That you prioritize your best-performing content.

Most publishers use composite engagement scores to promote the right behaviors. Almost everyone is de-prioritizing traffic and promoting quality engagement – session duration, clicks, scroll depth, comments – because they believe this is the key to building a truly engaged audience.

You could use statistical modeling, potentially supported by AI, to identify articles that are over-performing against expected engagement levels (a Bayesian like approach), then surface them more prominently across homepages, section fronts and topic pages.

Rather than relying exclusively on editorial judgement, you’re combining it with actual audience behavior.

The result should be a better user experience and more opportunities to generate clicks.

Keep it really, really simple and set clear key performance indicators.

CTR is not a perfect measure of success. Often, the most clickable content can veer into rage-bait or clickbait. Which is exactly the kind of content you want to avoid.

As referral traffic becomes harder to generate, publishers need to make more of the audiences they already have. This is a simple, almost certainly effective way of doing that.

SEO Director at The Telegraph with a decade of experience. Unskilled jiu-jitsu guy. Average chess player. Jack of many things.

#7407. Instagram offers to pay creators for long-form content

Creators were offered as much as $500,000 to post videos to Meta’s new Features product, according to Business Insider.

Instagram is reportedly offering some creators up to $500,000 to post long-form content as it looks for new ways to drive interest in its latest content experiment.

Last week, Instagram announced initial testing of its new Features option, which will let creators post videos of up to 90 minutes.

Yet despite providing the capacity to share longer videos, creators don’t have much incentive to do so because YouTube’s Partner Program offers significantly more potential to earn money from that content.

Instagram Chief Adam Mosseri has acknowledged this, and said in a Threads post on Thursday that “the economics matter a ton,” and that he and his team are exploring multiple options to help long-form creators get paid.

According to Business Insider, the first effort on that front is a series of one-off payments to big-name stars.

As per BI: “The Meta-owned platform has offered select creators $50,000 to post long-form video to its new ‘Features’ product, while others have been offered six-figure deals, some of which have reached $500,000.”

Meta didn’t confirm or deny the details. Instead, a Meta spokesperson told Business Insider that Meta is “funding Features content directly with some creators,” and that it’s “excited to see the content going live."

Which suggests that this will be a significant part of its new long-form push. Though Meta should know better than anyone that this type of upfront payment process won’t drive ongoing interest or adoption.

That’s because Meta has done this before. The company offered creators big money deals in the past to post to its long-form content option IGTV.

As reported by SMT sister publication Marketing Dive in 2021, Meta said it would pay out $1 billion to creators in 2022, including special payments to creators who allowed ads in their IGTV content, in order to boost interest in posting long-form videos in the app.

That didn’t raise user interest in the option, and by early 2022, Meta was already moving to shut down the separate IGTV app.

Other platforms have tried similar one-off payment approaches to entice creators to post longer clips. Earlier this year X offered a $175k prize for users who used Grok to generate their own version of “The Odyssey” movie.

Generally, these types of promotions lead to a spike in posts related to the promotion. But in most cases, they don’t spark a sustainable increase in video content posting or consumption.

Maybe Instagram has a better plan in place this time, but without a sustainable revenue share system in place, it’s hard to see the platform’s new long-form option appealing to a lot of creators.

#7405. Your buyers are sending agents. Will they recommend your brand?

For most of my career, marketing and sales have run on a single assumption: a person is paying attention. We wrote for human eyes, optimized for human clicks, and designed every interaction for human discernment. That assumption is starting to break.

Buyers are sending AI agents out ahead of them that have been tasked to read what you publish, compare you against the field, and hand the user a shortlist, often before that user lands on your site or talks to sales. At the same time, marketing and sales teams are building their own AI systems to keep pace. Both shifts are landing at once, and together they change how demand gets created and how trust gets earned.

This is the biggest shift in marketing since search itself. The brands that come out ahead will be the ones that get their foundation right first, because there is no AI strategy without a data strategy.

We have optimized for machines before; that was the whole idea behind search. What is happening now is a different order of magnitude. Agents are constantly reading, comparing, and recommending next steps based on users’ prompts.

That changes my job and yours. An agent now reads our story, determines whether our brand looks clear and trustworthy, and uses that read to decide whether we make the shortlist it hands to a decision-maker. Clear, consistent, well-structured content is what tips that decision and most brands will never know when they have been left off the list.

Enterprise marketing has always been episodic. We run a campaign, pass a lead, let sales run a play, and start over. Agent-driven buyers no longer move on that calendar, so our engines must evolve.

The shift now is toward an always-on system that reads each customer continuously, puts the best opportunities first, and evolves in the moment. This new process is rewiring how marketing and sales work together, from a handoff between two teams into one operation looking at the same customer at the same time.

But none of it works without a trustworthy foundation. If revenue or pipeline means six different things in six systems, layering AI on top will only scale the confusion. Governed, well-understood data is what lets an always-on engine move fast without getting reckless. When everyone has access to the same models, data becomes your differentiator because customer history and context are yours alone.

There is of course a grim version of this future as well. Infinite personalization collapses into infinite noise and every inbox fills with confident, hollow outreach. I think that outcome is a choice, not an inevitability.

Used well, AI takes the mechanical work off our teams: the assembling, formatting, and first draft nobody enjoys. When it gives back time, that time is only worth something if people spend it where machines cannot: understanding a customer, building a real relationship, making the call on what comes next.

That does not happen by accident. It takes leaders willing to rewrite roles, retrain teams, and give people room to experiment. But the judgment, creativity, and taste required to plan and execute differentiated marketing at scale will always remain human.

Your next customer may arrive as an agent or send one ahead. When that agent builds its shortlist, I want Snowflake on it, and I want your company on it too. That is the work in front of every marketing and sales leader now.

#7403. AI Shopping: Get Your Products Found In Google & ChatGPT via @sejournal, @hethr_campbell

Spend less time on publishing and reports, and more on helpful content. November 18, 2 PM ET.

Invalid traffic on AI Max search doubled to 5.28%. Get the channel-by-channel retail benchmarks before Black Friday.

See how Search Engine Journal checks a real site for crawler access and ranks fixes by business impact.

Explore Ahrefs’ Q2, Q3 2026 AI search benchmarks and practical next steps.

Ask the SEJ experts you already read what AI, agents, and algorithm changes mean for your traffic. Private, human to human. $97/mo.

seoClarity's Chris Sachs and Aris Vrakas explained how readable pages, shopper language, consistent product feeds, and testing support AI shopping discovery.

A product can have the right material, price, and features and still give an AI shopping tool too little context to recommend it. The challenge is not simply getting a feed accepted. It is making product information retrievable, relevant to shopper needs, and consistent wherever it appears.

In this webinar, AI Shopping: Get Your Products Found In Google & ChatGPT, Chris Sachs, VP of Value Enablement, and Aris Vrakas, Product Lead at seoClarity, walked through four connected moves: make product pages readable, enrich content in shopper language, synchronize feeds and pages, and measure what changed.

The session focused on work ecommerce teams could do now, rather than waiting for checkout inside AI services to mature.

Vrakas began with a technical prerequisite: product content needed to be available to bots, not only visible to someone using a browser. He described JavaScript-heavy pages where shoppers could see details that an automated fetch did not expose.

For teams reviewing their stores, that distinction calls for checking what a crawler actually receives. Are core product details present in the HTML? Does important information depend on client-side execution? Where rendering was a bottleneck, Vrakas discussed prerendering and cached HTML as options.

His point was to address access before investing heavily in enrichment. Better descriptions cannot help a system that never receives them.

The presenters used socks as a recurring example. Color and fiber composition are useful attributes, but they do not answer every question about warmth, thickness, durability, or intended use. Vrakas explained that conversational prompts could combine several of those needs in one request.

He recommended connecting product characteristics with the decisions shoppers were trying to make. Rather than leaving an AI model to infer a use case from a short specification, retailers could provide relevant details directly. He also discussed using attributable review evidence to support claims about how a product performed.

The opportunity was not limited to unusually long prompts. Vrakas explained that even a broad request for a good product could lead an AI system to consider more specific attributes. The webinar’s product-enrichment discussion explored how retailers could anticipate those questions without relying on generic descriptions.

Sachs warned against treating the product feed as a separate project owned somewhere else in the organization. Information added to a feed should also inform the product page and its structured data, rather than stopping at the feed.

Vrakas described why that consistency mattered when a platform revisited a product page: product identity, price, and supporting details needed to be clear and coherent. An enriched feed did not remove the need to make the corresponding page understandable.

The presenters called this closing the loop. For an ecommerce team, it meant connecting SEO, merchandising, paid media, and development so that one channel’s improvements could benefit another, instead of maintaining disconnected versions of the same product information.

The presenters separated three areas of measurement:

These were different signals, not interchangeable definitions of success. An AI mention alone did not establish a sale, and the presenters encouraged teams to examine the effects across the channels they were changing.

Chris Sachs tied that measurement work to ongoing experimentation:

“The best SEO teams are the ones that obsess about testing. The more testing you do, the better decision making you can have.”

During the Q&A, a viewer asked whether a before-and-after comparison was enough to evaluate generative engine optimization. Vrakas distinguished large catalogs, where product groups could support comparisons, from smaller sites or broader brand questions. For the latter, he described improving content around a defined topic and comparing its movement with other topics left untouched.

The useful distinction was having a comparison set, rather than assuming that every visibility change proved an optimization had worked.

The full on-demand session connected technical retrieval, feed enrichment, and measurement examples. To turn the discussion into a first review with your team:

#7402. Express unwraps holiday campaign to build on brand metric gains

Let Marketing Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

CMO Joe Berean explains how “Holiday, Hamlin Style” was inspired by the organic social engagement of the retailer’s spring campaign.

Express has enlisted a family of pop cultural figures for its holiday campaign, according to details shared with Marketing Dive. The “Holiday, Hamlin Style” effort reimagines holiday traditions and unites actor-model-reality TV star Lisa Rinna; her husband, actor Harry Hamlin; her daughter, model Delilah Belle Hamlin; and Delilah’s boyfriend, “Love Island” veteran Mick Maio.

Rinna, who began her career as a model and on soap operas before becoming a breakout star on “The Real Housewives of Beverly Hills,” has a long-standing place in pop culture and connects with different consumer groups in different ways. She also has displayed a gift for virality, whether on reality TV or competition shows including “Dancing with the Stars” and “The Traitors.”

“That connection that she has to people just really comes through so authentically and translates so well into today's social media era. That's exactly what we captured on the campaign, too,” Express CMO Joe Berean said. “We designed it as one that can have that polish of a traditional holiday campaign, but really be social-first.”

The social-first push is in line with Express’ other marketing efforts, like the Expressionists, its community of creators, associates and brand ambassadors that is helping to bring to life “Dress to Express,” a brand platform centered around instilling confidence and inspiring self-expression.

Express in August launched a fall campaign, “Too Easy to Look This Good,” that featured NFL player Tee Higgins and pop star Tinashe, weeks before her album “Popstar” took the charts and the pop culture world by storm. The campaign generated the brand’s highest earned media and highest paid media traffic to its website for the year so far. From Q2 into Q3, Express saw brand awareness increase 3% and consideration increase 2%, Berean said.

“We're looking to move the needle in terms of bringing our brand back, reigniting the heat of the brand at its heyday and even exceeding that,” Berean said. “We've seen this sequential improvement of awareness and consideration, which is exactly the intent, especially as we're going into this crowded holiday season.”

Express has been looking to reheat its brand since filing for Chapter 11 in April 2024. The retailer and its Bonobos subsidiary are owned by Phoenix Retail, a joint venture from Simon Property Group, Brookfield Properties, Centennial and brand management firm WHP Global.

The campaign is Express’ latest effort that seeks to build buzz for the brand with celebrity-led activations, and arrives as the retailer works to engage both its existing customer base and a new generation of shoppers.

“The intent of the holiday campaign is to break through during a very crowded holiday season, make a splash and have it be scroll-stopping with Lisa's one-liners and dance moves and this really loving, authentic and stylish family dynamic that only they can bring,” Berean said.

“Holiday, Hamlin Style” follows a successful spring campaign that also featured Belle Hamlin and Maio. “It Couple, It Looks” launched in April and delivered the brand’s highest organic social engagement of the year so far, the retailer reported. In addition, Express said it has seen success with the multigenerational marketing approach in a Mother’s Day campaign starring model Karolína Kurková and her daughter.

“We've been trying different tactics to reach new customers… and those customers span generations,” Berean said of the holiday campaign. “To have a total family dynamic with mother, daughter, father and boyfriend was strategic, in that sense, to appeal to a wide audience.”

Shot in Los Angeles, the holiday campaign includes a series of seasonal vignettes and is set to remixes of Tchaikovsky’s “The Nutcracker.” It will run across video, social, digital and in-store channels throughout the season, highlighting styles priced from $38 to $298. The campaign arrives as big box retailers Target and Walmart and retail brands including Anthropologie and Sephora launch holiday campaigns.

“The competition is starting earlier. It's getting more aggressive. So having talent and campaigns that can really connect with people and break through is the name of the game,” Berean said.

#7401. Engineering Manager, Automation Intelligence at Fingerprint

Fingerprint empowers enterprises to detect and stop online fraud with the world’s most accurate device intelligence.  We lead our industry with bleeding-edge identification capabilities and work on turning new ideas and discoveries in the fraud detection space into reality. Our customers range from innovative startups to leading enterprise companies, including Plaid, Dropbox, and Booking.com.

Fingerprint is a globally dispersed, 100% remote company. We were named on on the 2026 Forbes Best Startup Employers list and ranked #803 on the 2026 Inc. 5000 list of America’s fastest-growing private companies.

We have raised $77M and are backed by Craft Ventures (Tesla, Facebook, Airbnb ), Nexus Venture Partners ( Postman, Apollo.io, MinIO, Druva) and Uncorrelated Ventures ( Redis, Rollbar,  Gradle).

Fingerprint's device intelligence platform processes billions of identification events for companies that need to know who — or what — is on the other end of a request. Increasingly, the answer is not a person. AI agents, AI assistants, crawlers, headless browsers, and malicious automation now make up a large and fast-growing share of internet traffic, and telling them apart from humans — and from each other — is becoming one of the most important problems our customers ask us to solve.

We are standing up a dedicated Automation Intelligence engineering team to own that problem end to end: detection in the browser, detection at the edge, the classification taxonomy that tells customers what kind of automation they're seeing and how confident we are, and the API that customers and their agents call.

We're looking for an Engineering Manager to lead this team. You'll inherit a small, senior group (three engineers to start, growing to four or five) and a clear mandate. You report directly to the VP of Engineering.

This is a hands-on-adjacent engineering manager role for a small, senior, high-leverage team. You are not expected to write the production code — your engineers and their agents do that — but you are expected to be close enough to it to set direction, evaluate the work, and hold the quality bar.

The team has unusual autonomy and unusual visibility. It owns a product that leadership considers strategically critical, it is the first team operating Fingerprint's AI-native engineering model, and its results — good and bad — will be used as evidence for how the rest of engineering works. That means two things: you'll have real room to decide how the team operates, and you'll need to be honest about what the data shows.

In the first 90 days you will likely: finalize the roster and ownership lines with the sibling team, get the quality loop and engineering baselines running, deliver the engineering evidence for the Beta gate, and run the first review of the AI-native operating model.

At Fingerprint, we believe in pay transparency — it's part of how we operate as a distributed, remote-first team.  Salaries are based on function, level, and geographic location, benchmarked against similar stage growth companies.

Because this role is open across the United States and can be based anywhere our team members work from, the salary range reflects that geographic breadth: $159k - $215k

#7399. TikTok Search Ads Adds Brand And Category Keyword Packs via @sejournal, @MattGSouthern

Explore Ahrefs’ Q2, Q3 2026 AI search benchmarks and practical next steps.

Ask the SEJ experts you already read what AI, agents, and algorithm changes mean for your traffic. Private, human to human. $97/mo.

AI Search Tracking Checklist: How to Attribute Calls From ChatGPT, Claude, and Perplexity

TikTok is highlighting two impression share metrics for Search Ads and adding curated keyword packs. Here's what each does.

TikTok’s Product Preview of Q4 2026 shows two impression share metrics for Search Ads Campaigns, and TikTok is adding curated keyword groups called Keyword Packs to their Keyword Suggestion Tool.

The two share metrics are designed to measure how much of the eligible search inventory a campaign is taking advantage of, and TikTok’s guide to low shares begins by asking if the campaign has already exhausted its budget.

TikTok’s help page defines these metrics as follows:

Impression Share: The percentage of eligible search results impressions your ads captured.

Top 1 Impression Share: The percentage of eligible first-position search results impressions your ads captured.

The Oct. 30 post describes them in auction terms, as showing “the percentage of eligible search auctions an advertiser’s keywords successfully won on the Search Results Page.” Neither page explains how TikTok counts the eligible opportunities a campaign didn’t win.

The post says the metrics are available at the campaign, advertiser, and keyword levels in TikTok Ads Manager. The help page says Ads Manager may show them for eligible Search Ads Campaign and TopSearch campaigns. TopSearch, a CPM-billed Search Ads option, places an ad in the first sponsored position for a keyword when it wins the auction.

Availability can differ depending on your account, campaign type, market, and product rollout status, as noted on the help page.

According to TikTok’s Help Page, if a campaign has a low impression share, then based upon other factors (budget, bidding, keyword coverage, creative relevance, competition level, and/or fluctuation of search volume), there could be an opportunity to capture additional eligible impressions.

The troubleshooting section of the help page uses spending to distinguish between the first two. If a campaign spends all of its available money, the help page recommends increasing the campaign’s budget to see if you are able to capitalize on more eligible impressions. However, when a campaign does not spend its available funds, the help page recommends checking whether your bid is competitive for those same keywords. The rest of the suggestions include reviewing your keyword list for opportunities to add new ones as well as making sure that your creative and landing pages match your keywords.

Neither the Help Page nor the Reporting Guide published by TikTok in April provided information on metrics to measure how much of a campaign’s impression share was being lost due to budget or ranking.

TikTok says its Keyword Suggestion Tool “can now recommend” Keyword Packs to advertisers, “ready-made, curated” based on brand and category keywords. Advertisers can preview and add these suggested packs using the same workflow used today. In addition, according to TikTok, the pack suggestions are refreshed each day, utilizing metrics including (but not limited to) search volume, cost-per-click (CPC) and competition, allowing advertisers to utilize keyword lists “backed by TikTok’s machine learning models.”

Brooke Osmundson wrote about the inclusion of keyword suggestions within Search Ads setup when it first launched in the U.S. in September 2024. The current version of the help section, which was last revised in November 2025, does reference suggested keywords with monthly impression predictions and match types, but does so without referencing packs. Also, TikTok provides a different Keyword Planner, which contains information regarding search volume and competition for advertising planners wishing to develop their own keyword lists.

The post doesn’t say which markets or accounts can use Keyword Packs, or how TikTok chooses the terms in a brand pack.

Where a share metric appears in your TikTok account, go to the keyword-level view to find keywords getting only a small percentage of their eligible impressions. Once you have identified those terms, then proceed with checking if your campaign has fully exhausted its budget using TikTok’s troubleshooting steps.

For Keyword Packs, the preview TikTok describes is the place to review brand terms before adding them.

The October 30th post and TikTok’s Search Ads overview are the only areas I could locate on the site regarding Keyword Packs. TikTok’s suggestion tool help page, which was last updated in November 2025, will be an area to keep an eye on for market and account information as well. Until this page is updated, you can check Ads Manager to see if your account includes either of these features within reporting for Share Metrics and when setting up keywords for the Keyword Packs.

#7398. X launches new bonus program for video creators

The reward-based effort offers cash incentives to participants for posting videos on the platform.

X has launched a new program to entice video creators to post in the app, with its Video Kickstart program offering cash incentives for users who post a lot of video clips each month.

As explained by X: “The Video Kickstart Bonus is an invite-only pilot program that pays creators a bonus for posting their own short-form, vertical videos on X. Post 15 qualifying videos on 15 different days during a program period, and you earn the bonus for that period.”

Each video needs to be shot in vertical format and be under three minutes long to qualify. X added that videos must be original, though creators will be allowed to post videos they’ve previously shared to other platforms, “as long as they are yours.”

X said selected creators will be invited into the program and need to enroll in the program to get a share of bonus payments.

“The program runs in periods of about one month,” X said. “Each period has an enrollment deadline and a last day to post, and you can find both in your invitation and in Creator Studio.”

X also said that creators who sign up for the program will also be fast-tracked into its Original Content Rewards Program, through which they’ll be able to earn a percentage of any ads shown in relation to their posts.

X has been trying to make video a bigger focus in the app, in order to better align with content consumption trends and get people spending more time in the X feed.

In July, the platform launched an updated livestream studio process and offered $1 million in funding for livestream creators to help raise awareness of the option. In August, X added new video overlay options, and last month it rolled out an updated video composer, which includes a range of improved creative options.

In 2023, X reported that video consumption was rapidly rising in the app. The company said it would be working to improve its video presentation tools to align with this shift.

X is lagging behind a little in getting video posting options out there, but as more people spend time consuming video content, X is clearly looking to draw an increasing amount of video content from its users.

#7397. NY Times Accuses Brave Of Scraping And Exploiting Content via @sejournal, @martinibuster

Spend less time on publishing and reports, and more on helpful content. November 18, 2 PM ET.

Invalid traffic on AI Max search doubled to 5.28%. Get the channel-by-channel retail benchmarks before Black Friday.

See how Search Engine Journal checks a real site for crawler access and ranks fixes by business impact.

Explore Ahrefs’ Q2, Q3 2026 AI search benchmarks and practical next steps.

Ask the SEJ experts you already read what AI, agents, and algorithm changes mean for your traffic. Private, human to human. $97/mo.

AI Search Tracking Checklist: How to Attribute Calls From ChatGPT, Claude, and Perplexity

The New York Times is suing the makers of Brave Browser for unauthorized copying of journalism and copyright infringement for allegedly scraping publisher content to sell to AI companies and enabling customers to bypass its paywall. Although Brave positions itself as a white company that benefits consumers, the lawsuit paints a picture of a black hat company that exploits the work of publishers.

Brave Browser and Brave Search positions themselves as the “good guys” on the Internet by casting themselves as the solution to an Internet that’s broken by surveillance capitalism. It positions itself as the antidote to Google’s aggressive monetization of its own users.

The New York Times’ copyright infringement lawsuit against the makers of Brave Browser and Search Engine reveals a darker reality behind the gleaming “user-first” stance in which Brave is alleged to exploit the work of journalists without compensation.

The New York Times lawsuit is a challenge to an alternative business model that instead of exploiting its users the way Google does it exploits the work of publishers on the internet by selling access to that content to AI companies, and uses it to provide answers that compete with the publishers’ own websites.

Brave allegedly provides unauthorized copies of Times journalism through its Search API, allowing third parties to use that content without obtaining licenses from The Times. The content that Brave scrapes off publisher sites is alleged to be sold to AI companies which then can use it to train their large language models. That same content is also sold to be used by AI Chat as grounding content that’s surfaced through retrieval-augmented generation (RAG).

The complaint cites Brave’s own marketing of its Search API for AI training, grounding, and content extraction as evidence that Brave knowingly promotes uses that The Times considers infringing. This is part of the complaint’s allegation that Brave deliberately created its products to exploit copyrighted content by making it available to third parties, including creating features that bypass publishers’ paywalls.

The inference that can be made here is that if Brave is doing this to The New York Times, it’s likely doing it to other websites that publish content on the Internet, whether it’s a recipe site, a product review site, or any other  kind of website that earns income from showing advertising.

The partnership will expand the existing relationship and include livestream women’s tennis coverage as well as behind-the-scenes insights.

TikTok announced a new partnership with the WTA, which will see the app host exclusive women’s tennis content and establish new tournament coverage hubs in order to help drive more engagement in the app.

This deal formalizes TikTok’s partnership with the WTA.

The livestream will set the stage for the season-ending championship taking place November 8–15, where fans will see the world’s top eight singles players and doubles teams will compete for one of the most prestigious titles in women’s tennis.”

The WTA will also work with TikTok to create a new “WTA Creator Club,” which is designed to engage digital creators in WTA coverage. The program will feature opportunities for creators to access tournaments around the world.

“Participating creators will capture and share the sport through unique viewpoints, offering fans an authentic look at life on Tour, from behind-the-scenes moments to the stories beyond the court,” TikTok said.

There will also be new WTA content access powered by TikTok’s GamePlan offering, which will bring together related content for tournaments and events.

“Player-led storytelling will remain a central focus, with the WTA and TikTok working together to help more athletes build their presence on the platform,” TikTok said. “WTA stars are already using TikTok to share their personalities, interests and experiences beyond competition, connecting directly with fans and helping audiences across sport, fashion, lifestyle and entertainment discover women's tennis.”

Sports content has become a bigger focus for TikTok, with a rising number of fans now turning to the app for insights and highlights.

In 2024, TikTok published a report that found that 59% of its users thought watching sports content in the app was more entertaining than watching the actual games.

TikTok also announced a content partnership with the ATP last year.

We're looking for a Senior Manager, Application Security to lead that investment, reporting directly to the VP of Engineering. You will personally own and build the AppSec program, and you will also manage a small team of experienced practitioners who run IT operations and compliance.

Cross-functional Leadership & Communication

This role is AppSec-first with a management scope that spans IT and compliance. That shape is deliberate. Application security is where we're investing next, and we want someone who can personally build it — not just oversee it. At the same time, IT and compliance are already run by experienced practitioners who don't need a domain expert over them; they need a leader who gives them direction, context, and prioritization.

What this means in practice: you will be hands-on in AppSec and hands-off-but-accountable in IT and compliance. We don't expect you to arrive as an expert in IT operations or GRC. We do expect you to learn those domains quickly — well enough within your first few months to evaluate your team's work, make prioritization calls across all three disciplines, and represent each of them credibly to leadership and customers. The judgment to know when to rely on your team vs. when to drive the decision yourself is what distinguishes the right candidate.

This is a role for someone who is still a strong practitioner in AppSec and has grown into leading people — not for someone who has moved entirely out of technical work, and not for a pure individual contributor who hasn't managed before.
