Our team is a quickly growing group of committed researchers, engineers, policy experts, and business leaders working together to build beneficial AI systems.

Claude is trained and served on hardware in data centers Anthropic operates and in sites partners build and run for us. DCSE secures that footprint: the sites, the hardware moving through them, and the people and vendors who touch it. You'll be the engineer who owns autonomous systems across that footprint as one security domain, from both sides: drones, ground robots and automated vehicles for patrol, inspection and moving hardware, including the security of that automation inside secure areas, and the detection systems that tell us what is flying over or operating near a site uninvited.

You set the approach, select and integrate the platforms and sensors, write the requirements vendors build to, and run what you ship, from the operating model where operators assign missions and machines carry them out to the playbook that turns a detection into an identified track and a well-evidenced handoff to whoever holds authority over the airspace. Interdiction is not part of this role. The goal is security that runs itself: coverage, fleet health and evidence verified continuously, with tools and AI agents doing the routine triage. We favor simple, standard controls and tools that can be verified over elaborate ones. We use Claude throughout our engineering workflow and expect you to as well.

You'll work with the rest of DCSE, including its physical security systems engineers and the engineers on its security monitoring platform, with Anthropic's data center operations and legal teams, and directly with site operators, equipment vendors and aviation and public safety authorities. The role is based in San Francisco, with remote applicants in the US considered, and involves regular travel to data center sites, which are critical infrastructure.

Autonomous security and inspection systems: own the drones, ground robots and other autonomous platforms for perimeter patrol, incident response and infrastructure inspection: platform selection, operating model, operator qualification, maintenance, and the data path from machine to analyst

Tools, automation and AI agents: build the integrations that carry detections, tracks, telemetry and fleet health into the team's security monitoring platform, and the tools and agents that triage detections, check fleet health and assemble evidence. When a review keeps costing people time, you build the tool or agent that retires it

Detection of uncrewed systems: engineer the sensing that tells us what is flying over or operating near a site, what it is and who needs to know: sensor selection and evaluation against live targets, per-site coverage, classification and false-alarm handling, and the playbook from first detection to handoff, with evidence retained

Security of site automation: engineer the controls on automated vehicles, robots and similar machines that move hardware or work inside secure areas: identity and access, network isolation, what they may record and where it goes, fail-secure behavior, their place in chain of custody, and acceptance before they enter a site

Health monitoring: define and test heartbeats, loss-of-coverage and lost-link alarms and fail-secure behavior, so a silent sensor or machine shows as a fault, not a quiet day

Vendor requirements and acceptance: write the requirements, evaluate against live flights and runs, and hold vendors to what they ship

Have engineered autonomous or sensing systems in the field (drones, ground robots, automated vehicles, or RF, radar or optical detection sensors): selected, integrated or secured them for an operation other people relied on, and verified what they actually do against real targets or runs rather than a datasheet

Have integrated systems you did not build (vendor APIs, SDKs, appliances, machine and sensor telemetry) into a platform that outlives any one of them

Read and review code, design the data systems behind security tooling, and build automation (often with AI coding agents) using Git and code review

Have done this work hands-on for data centers, critical infrastructure or a large hardware fleet

Own a domain end to end: set the approach, carry it through with vendors, site operators, partners and authorities, and run it as something people depend on every day

Remote pilot certification (for example FAA Part 107) and experience flying beyond visual line of sight under a waiver or exemption

Experience with counter-drone (counter-UAS) detection, airspace surveillance or air traffic data systems

Experience running a robot, drone or automated-vehicle fleet in an industrial, logistics or data center setting: fleet management, mission planning, safety cases

Experience securing embedded, operational technology or robotic systems: device identity, network segmentation, update control, logging

Experience with physical security operations at large sites, with law enforcement or aviation authorities, or at sites you did not fully control: cloud, colocation or GPU cloud operators

Have built AI agents or automation that replaced recurring manual work

#7393. Google Lighthouse Update Changes How PageSpeed Insights Simulates Loading via @sejournal, @MattGSouthern

See how Search Engine Journal checks a real site for crawler access and ranks fixes by business impact.

Invalid traffic on AI Max search doubled to 5.28%. Get the channel-by-channel retail benchmarks before Black Friday.

See how Search Engine Journal checks a real site for crawler access and ranks fixes by business impact.

Explore Ahrefs’ Q2, Q3 2026 AI search benchmarks and practical next steps.

Ask the SEJ experts you already read what AI, agents, and algorithm changes mean for your traffic. Private, human to human. $97/mo.

AI Search Tracking Checklist: How to Attribute Calls From ChatGPT, Claude, and Perplexity

A Google Lighthouse update changes how PageSpeed Insights simulates page loads, which can raise lab scores on pages that haven't changed.

PageSpeed Insights is now using Lighthouse 13.6.0, which was released Oct. 27. This version of Lighthouse changes how it simulates how long a page takes to load.

Chrome’s Web Performance Developer Advocate, Barry Pollard, has stated on Bluesky that the updated version of PageSpeed Insights with Lighthouse 13.6.0 is now available. He said this improves the simulated FCP (First Contentful Paint) and the simulated LCP (Largest Contentful Paint), both of which Pollard says will be more accurate than before and generally result in higher scores.

A page may receive a different PageSpeed Insights score without anything on it changing, due to the change in the test. Pollard also said that websites heavily dependent on JavaScript would be more likely to see an impact from the updated test.

Pollard quoted a developer who had posted about how single-page apps (SPAs) had seen a big improvement in their results since the Lighthouse update, citing react.dev. He opened his post stating, “True!”, then added:

“Note this is not SPA-specific (and unrelated to other SPA things we’ve been working on), but more likely to impact JS-heavy sites.”

The experimental soft navigation metrics in Chrome that I wrote about back in October are a separate way of measuring user experience. The data comes from real Chrome users through the Chrome User Experience Report (CrUX) and Lighthouse uses a simulated test.

By default, Lighthouse will load a page at full speed and then estimate how it would load on a slower connection. The throttling docs say that this simulated throttling matches PageSpeed Insights’ setup.

The release notes for version 13.6.0 of Lighthouse list several changes to Lantern, which is the simulation behind those estimates, aimed at improving its accuracy.

Lighthouse 13.5 used each request’s fetch priority to decide whether or not it blocked rendering. In 13.6, Lantern uses Chrome’s own render-blocking label for each request (and falls back to using priority only when that label is missing).

A second change applies when the LCP element is text that appears in the same frame as the page’s first content. Lantern now estimates that LCP from the same requests and page tasks it uses for FCP instead of from a set that can include every request made before LCP.

Ivan Akulov showed in a GitHub issue how the old simulation handled a test page created with Framer whose only content above the fold was text, so one element counted as both FCP and LCP. Lighthouse reported much later LCP than FCP for that text. Even though preloading the page’s JavaScript modules improved interactive time for the real page, they caused simulated LCP to be worse.

Pollard thanked Akulov in a reply, saying he “kicked off some of these improvements.” The Lighthouse team closed the issue as completed in the Lantern upgrade listed in the 13.6 release notes.

Lighthouse 13.6 has also updated the Agentic Resource Discovery (ARD) audit from Lighthouse 13.5, which I covered in September. When Lighthouse 13.5 was released, it looked for an ARD manifest under the old ai-catalog names even though the spec had changed to ard.json. So if your website only used the new name but you didn’t include a line in your robots.txt file called Agentmap, it would show as Not Applicable.

With this update, Lighthouse 13.6 will look for an Agentmap line, a rel="ard" link tag or Link header and /.well-known/ard.json prior to looking for the ai-catalog names. And if Lighthouse 13.6 finds the manifest only at an ai-catalog location, it will note that ARD consumers are not required to check there.

The performance score listed for your page in PageSpeed Insights is based on data generated by Lighthouse. According to Google’s PageSpeed Insights documentation, lab data are results of a simulated load using a single device running on a fixed set of network conditions. They note that “having good lab data does not necessarily mean real-user experiences will also be good.”

Field data (also shown in the same report) represents real visits to your website within the last 28 days, as reported by CrUX. Each PageSpeed Insights report includes the version of Lighthouse used to generate results, next to the emulated device.

PageSpeed Insights scores from before and after the Lighthouse 13.6 update weren’t measured with the same simulation. Part of a higher lab score on a page you just optimized may come from the new version, and charts that track lab scores over time may show a jump at the switch to 13.6.

Before reporting an improvement, check that both reports name the same Lighthouse version, and compare the field data.

Lighthouse’s release notes say its team expects 13.6 to ship in the DevTools of Chrome 159, which is now in Chrome’s desktop Dev channel and in Canary. As of Nov. 2, Chrome’s release schedule lists Chrome 159 for stable release on Dec. 8.

An open issue on Lighthouse’s GitHub is also looking into cases where Lantern’s simulated LCP runs far above measured LCP, using large JavaScript and SPA sites as examples.

#7392. Threads adds more community champion badges

The digital display cards are designed to highlight users who have become key contributors in their topical subgroups.

Threads has added new community champion badges to recognize top contributors in topical communities, while it's also doubling the number of recognized champions in communities.

As shown in this example, posted by NBA Threads champion Brian Moller, key contributors in Threads communities will be rewarded with these new digital, flippable badges, which they'll then be able to share with others in the app to highlight their community engagement.

As explained by Threads: “Champions make their communities feel like home, and earn their badges by bringing people together and keeping the conversation going.”

Threads said that community champions will be able to manage flair and custom reactions for their communities. Threads added custom flair tags to communities in July.

In a separate post, Threads Chief Connor Hayes said that communities have become "a central part" of Threads as more people come to the app to participate in topical discussion.

Hayes added that Threads wants to build on community engagement by adding more topic groups including NWSL, PWHL and NFL fanbases, as well as location-based groups, such as ohiothreads and memphis.

Launched in September 2025, Threads communities provide an easier way for users to find their people by bringing together like-minded people in searchable, topic-based discussions.

In June, as part of Meta’s announcement that Threads had reached 500 million users, the company said that communities was one of the fastest-growing features in the app.

#7391. Meta updates creator assistant chatbot

The company’s artificial intelligence-powered tool now features opportunity alerts, better help for content creation and automated replies.

Meta announced some updates to its artificial intelligence-powered creator assistant chatbot, including opportunity alerts, improved content creation guidance and automated comment replies.

Launched in June, Meta’s creator assistant provides AI-powered guidance on opportunities across Facebook and Instagram, based on an automated analysis of what’s working in each app.

The new features will help to extend Meta’s AI guidance and offer more opportunities to improve content performance and tap into key trends.

First, creator assistant will be able to proactively alert creators to relevant trends in their niche.

As shown in these images, creator assistant will now highlight key trends and potential opportunities.

Creator assistant can also offer in-depth guidance on content creation, such as detailed plans on how to create standout content.

As explained by Meta: “You can hand it an idea and get a storyboard and shot list back, or upload a draft before you post and get specific feedback on what's working or could be better. It puts together a starting point, and you decide what happens next — helping you speed through parts of your process that slow you down while you remain in control of your content.”

The tool can also highlight the most relevant questions from viewers in the post comment section and generate replies in the creator’s voice to streamline responses.

Creator assistant is also now available on the web, within Meta’s Creator Dashboard.

This will provide expanded capacity to integrate Meta’s AI-powered tools into different workflow setups. Meanwhile, Meta said it’s looking to bring creator assistant to more apps and tools where creators are putting together content and content plans.

The additional guidance could help creators generate more engagement across Meta’s apps. At the same time, there is always a risk of over-automating. With more tools available that tap into trends, it seems inevitable that originality will become an even more powerful differentiator.

#7390. Staff Application Security Engineer at Fingerprint

Fingerprint empowers enterprises to detect and stop online fraud with the world’s most accurate device intelligence.  We lead our industry with bleeding-edge identification capabilities and work on turning new ideas and discoveries in the fraud detection space into reality. Our customers range from innovative startups to leading enterprise companies, including Plaid, Dropbox, and Booking.com.

Fingerprint is a globally dispersed, 100% remote company. We were named on on the 2026 Forbes Best Startup Employers list and ranked #803 on the 2026 Inc. 5000 list of America’s fastest-growing private companies.

We have raised $77M and are backed by Craft Ventures (Tesla, Facebook, Airbnb ), Nexus Venture Partners ( Postman, Apollo.io, MinIO, Druva) and Uncorrelated Ventures ( Redis, Rollbar,  Gradle).

Fingerprint's device intelligence platform processes billions of identification events for some of the world's most security-conscious companies — financial institutions, fraud prevention operators, and enterprises that rely on us to stop attackers. That makes our own application security a product feature, not a back-office function: our customers' adversaries are, by definition, motivated to probe what we build.

As we scale with large enterprise customers, we're investing in application security as a dedicated, first-class engineering discipline. Security is already part of how our teams build — scanning in the pipeline, a Cloud Platform team that treats security as a partner, and a mature compliance posture — and we're now standing up a focused AppSec function, led by a Senior Manager, Application Security who owns the program and strategy, to take it to the next level. You will be the founding senior engineer of that function — the person who turns strategy into a working secure SDLC, finds and fixes what matters, and raises the security bar across engineering through technical depth, tooling, and influence.

This is a hands-on Staff role. You will design, build, and operate — not just advise.

Threat Modeling, Security Reviews & Vulnerability Management

Incident Response & Detection (application layer)

This is the senior individual-contributor counterpart to the Senior Manager, Application Security. The manager owns the program, the strategy, and the team; you own the technical depth and the execution. The two roles are designed to work as a pair: you'll bring the findings, the engineering judgment, and the ground truth from inside the codebase, and together you'll decide what to prioritize.

As a Staff engineer, you are expected to operate with a high degree of independence — scoping ambiguous problems, setting technical direction for AppSec, and driving multi-quarter initiatives without needing day-to-day direction. As the founding engineer of a dedicated AppSec function, the patterns, tooling, and standards you establish will be the foundation the practice grows on.

This is not a role for someone who wants to primarily write policy or manage a program from a distance. It's for an engineer who finds real bugs, builds real tooling, and earns the trust of other engineers by making their code better.

At Fingerprint, we believe in pay transparency — it's part of how we operate as a distributed, remote-first team.  Salaries are based on function, level, and geographic location, benchmarked against similar stage growth companies.

Because this role is open across the United States and can be based anywhere our team members work from, the salary range reflects that geographic breadth: $177k - $240k

#7389. What Reddit’s Q3 earnings mean for marketers amid major traffic shift

Let Marketing Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The platform’s ad revenue climbed 54% YoY, but signs of a slowdown persist.

Despite ad revenue growing 54% year-over-year, Reddit’s stock took a nosedive after the closing bell following the release of its Q3 earnings Thursday, largely due to slowed user growth and plunging referral traffic. Revenue overall grew 53% to $895 million YoY.

The social media platform, which went public in 2024, has been riding a wave of explosive growth driven by increased advertising capabilities and its high citation rate by artificial intelligence. However, as growth shows signs of tapering off and daily active users remain flat quarter-over-quarter, Reddit’s future is less clear.

Below, Marketing Dive pulled together three numbers from Reddit’s most recent earnings materials that marketers should be aware of as the platform becomes a bigger player in the online advertising space.

Reddit’s ad revenue growth outpaced overall revenue growth by a point. While strong, ad revenue made bigger gains earlier in the year and grew 64% YoY in Q2 and 74% YoY in Q1.

Click volume grew more than 50% in the most recent quarter and conversion volume more than tripled YoY, said Jen Wong, Reddit’s chief operating officer, during an earnings call. The platform forged deeper relationships with existing advertisers, with average spending and new advertiser acquisition both on the upswing, Wong reported. There was also a more than 50% YoY growth in mid-market advertisers in Europe, the Middle East and Africa, as well as North America.

As shoppers are increasingly bombarded by synthetic content, Reddit remains a go-to for those looking for a human opinion and half of U.S. shoppers verify AI recommendations on the platform, according to Wong. This makes Reddit a prime environment for advertisers looking to go after high-intent audiences.

“We see significant opportunity ahead for our business, and we're making progress reflected in strong customer retention, growing share of wallet and more always-on advertiser relationships. These trends give us confidence in Reddit's ability to deliver durable, differentiated growth,” said Wong, during the call.

The average number of referrals dropped per week in October

The number of U.S. daily users fell from 53.2 million in Q2 to 50.7 million in Q3 while international users rose from 77.1 million to 79.6 million, according to a shareholder letter.

This falloff can be, in-part, attributed to a sharp decrease in referral traffic. In October, referrals are falling by over one million per week on average. If the trend continues, Reddit’s daily active unique user count could fall by low double-digits next quarter, according to the letter. Changes to how consumers use AI overviews on Google, which continue to routinely cite Reddit, are in no small part behind the drop, according to Steven Huffman, co-founder and CEO of Reddit, during an earnings call.

“The reality is the AI Mode does not drive traffic, not for us and not to anybody as far as I know. So it's just a fundamental shift in how Google works,” said Huffman during the call. “It used to be a traffic driver. Now it's not.”

The platform has existing relationships with AI developers. Just before it went public in 2024, the platform signed a $60 million-a-year AI content deal with Google. A few months later, it entered into a similar deal with OpenAI, the company behind ChatGPT. Both deals are set to expire in 2027. Google is still “crawling” the platform “as hard as ever,” per Huffman. He also maintains that Reddit is one of the most Googled terms, meaning demand for the platform remains high.

To help combat user loss, the platform is shifting to a “logged-in only” experience, Huffman announced during the call. Logged-in users make up the bulk of Reddit’s engagement and are more valuable to advertisers, according to the co-founder. It will also help to protect the platform’s data.

The QoQ revenue growth of Reddit’s Ads API ecosystem

Reddit continues to build out its advertising tools. During the quarter, Reddit Max, an automated campaign offering, entered public beta, according to Wong. Automation is one area of focus for the company, along with expanding Reddit for Business and delivering increased value to advertisers.

Reddit is also seeing strength in video, with video ad revenue growth more than doubling YoY in Q3, according to the shareholder letter. During the quarter, the platform launched a 15-second engaged video view, building on the momentum of a six-second option. Retailer JCPenney saw a 4-point lift in ad awareness and a 90% higher completion rate with the longer videos compared to the shorter ones, per the letter.

#7388. Future of Marketing Briefing: SharkNinja’s McLaren deal treats F1 fandom as the asset

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This Future of Marketing Briefing covers the latest in marketing for Digiday+ members and is distributed over email every Friday at 10 a.m. ET. More from the series →

SharkNinja got a taste of F1 fandom when its products turned up in Brad Pitt’s racing film last year. Fans warmed to the brand and plenty finally twigged that Shark and Ninja were the same company. Then the buzz faded.

“The movie was great but it spiked and then it left,” said Laura Dyer, svp of global brand and partnerships at SharkNinja. “What’s great about this is it’s going to continue.”

“This” is a five-year sponsorship of McLaren and it got off to an early start. F1 sponsors tend to arrive over the winter, ready for their logos to be unveiled on the new car in February. SharkNinja turned up in mid-September, with most of the season already run, because it asked McLaren whether anything could go live before the year was out. McLaren said yes, which hands the brand a few months of practice ahead of 2027.

The practice matters since SharkNinja has never backed a sports property this big. Its previous sports deals were built around a single moment. At the 2025/1015 Women’s Rugby World CUp it was an official appliance partner, running standards at match venues, a fan zone at Battersea Power Station and a social series on matchday prep. A year earlier it put Thierry Henry, Peter Crouch and Bastian Schweinsteiger on a faux video call to squabble over who could host the best Euro 2024 watch party. Both were finished within weeks. McLaren, on the other hand, gave SharkNinja four racing series for five years. McLaren engineers in its labs and a direct line to drivers Lando Norris and Oscar Piastri.

That last part is what Dyer is most focused on. Where Norris and Piastri do appear, she wants them helping shape the idea, which is a change from the old sponsor routine of a driver turning up, reading the lines and leaving. “Getting them to do the things that they’re comfortable with is way better than handing someone a script and saying ‘there you go’,” Dyer continued. Fans follow the drivers as closely as the teams these days so keeping both onside matters. That goes double for a brand hoping to win those fans over on social media.

Most of that content will run on Mclaren’s channels and SharkNinja’s, and nothing goes live until both sides have signed it off. Even then, it won’t automatically land on Norris or Piastri’s accounts. “Their channels are theirs to own and theirs to editorialize,” said Dyer. If Norris or Piastri’s post about a blender, it’ll be because they fancied it.

That puts the onus on the content itself, though Dyer was frank that SharkNinja’s yardstick for it is still rough. Her answer on engagement, she warned, would be “really wooly.” Comments, shares and dwell time all go into the mix, weighed together, with reach a step behind. F1 and McLaren fans are fiercely loyal and Dyer said the brand is “really respectful of that”. Success as she put it, is a fan seeing a post and thinking. “‘oh yeah, like wicked. I totally get it’. Rather than this feels like advertising.”

Further out, SharNinja will track brand recognition and whether putting McLaren behind a specific product line shifts sales. As Dyer explained: “When we go hard at a product line with McLaren, we want to see whether or not that changes the trajectory of sales.”

Down the line, McLaren could even have a hand in the products as well the marketing. A set number of hours a year, the team’s engineers sit with SharkNina’s in Needham, Massachusetts, to “literally pen to paper solve problems together,” as Dyer put it. Battery life is one area they’ll dig into. Whether anything ends up with McLaren’s name on the box is an open question, and one Dyer didn’t answer. The engineering link was a big part of why SharkNinja picked McLaren, though. The brand is “really obsessive about the concept of marginal gains,” she said. “If we can get a battery to last like 50 seconds longer or get a fan to be 10% more efficient, we’ll do it.”

Sports sponsorship has been heading this way for a while. Live sport is one of the few remaining moments when a huge audience watches the same thing at once, and brands have been paying more to be part of it. What they’re buying has changed with the price. A logo on the hoardings and ad slots in the broadcast used to be the deal. These days marketers want to know what the fans themselves are worth, whether those fans follow the team or the people in its cars and kits.

“We operate more like an agency than a lot of traditional rights holders in that we insight on entering every conversation talking about the brand first,” said Ryan Fischer, president at Fenway Sports Management on an Advertising Week New York panel earlier this month. [We’re] understanding their objectives and strategies, then trying to identify the insight, whether that’s a consumer insight or a story we can help amplify, and letting that lead where we go within the portfolio rather than starting withoutselves.”

SharkNinja’s appearance in the F1 movie predated Dyer’s arrival but she was there to help execute it. Before it came out, she said “people saw us as Shark and Ninja”, said Dyer. Afterward, it was ‘Oh, they’re SharkNina,” she continued. The fan response caught the team off guard. “We were all pleasantly surprised by the sentiment and engagement with our brand in that movie by a fan base that I think really got us,” Dyer said. “That was definitely the inflection point to say okay, we should go further into the sport.”

The aim of it, like most sponsorships of this scale, is younger fans. Yes, F1’s global audience tops 900 million worldwide but the brand isn’t trying to carve that up — at least not yet. As Dyer put it: “We’re here for everyone, truthfully, and we want to stay for everyone.”

Forget the holiday season, it’s the Halloween campaigns you want to get right.

What’s scarier for a marketer: a campaign that fails to scare anyone, or a campaign that scares its audience so much they never want to buy the product again?

For Cheetos, apparently, the answer is a demon covered in orange snack dust.

The brand’s latest Halloween ad, “Man at The Bottom of The Bag” was ranked the ninth-scariest ad of all time by creative and media intelligence platform DAIVID. The spot, which is part of Cheetos’ Flavor of Fear collaboration with horror studio Blumhouse Productions, is predicted to leave 15.3% of viewers experiencing intense fear. Even HBO Max’s Halloween trailer, which promotes its season of spooky films, ranked 10th.

It turns out Halloween ads are (shockingly) losing some of their bite. This year’s campaigns were 32% less likely to provoke intense fear than those released in 2025 according to DAIVID — which means they’re less scary, sure, but not necessarily less effective.

DAIVID compiled the ranking using its AI-powered creative intelligence technology, which predicts the emotional responses audiences are likely to have towards ads, as well as its potential impact on brand and business metrics. And ads were ranked according to the percentage of viewers predicted to experience intense fear, defined as an emotional response score between eight and 10 on a 10-point scale.

Which begs the question, what came top of the all-time scary ads chart?

In first place was 2025’s I Choose Freedom campaign: “End The Horror”, which predicted 24.5% of viewers would feel intense fear after watching it.

Second place went to 2025’s Universal: “Dare To Answer The Black Phone”, which predicted 21.6% of viewers would experience intense fear.

Third place went to the Six Flags: “Tick, Tick, Tick” from 2024, where 19.5% of viewers were predicted to experience intense fear.

And let’s not forget Cheetos in ninth place, followed by HBO Max in 10th.

Still, there is some good news. The average Creative Effectiveness Score, according to DAIVID, sat at 5.84 out of 10 last year, and rose slightly to 5.85 for 2026.

Simply put, scaring the living daylights out of consumers can remain optional. But making the ad work is the real trick — or treat.

“Given that there is almost never only one perfect option for a decision, we’re confident that we can actually provide really interesting, compelling advertising models for agentic use cases… I’m really actually excited about the advertising opportunities in an agentic world.”

— Google’s svp and chief business officer Philipp Schindler, responding to an analyst question about the transition from search to agentic use during the company’s latest earnings call.

#7387. Companies may need to restrict AI use to manage costs

Anthropic, OpenAI, xAI and Meta have all spent enormous amounts of money to build out their artificial intelligence projects, but have yet to see returns.

The pressure is rising for artificial intelligence projects to show some level of return, with industry leaders OpenAI and Anthropic both posting massive losses in their earnings results, and xAI reducing access capacity due to non-viable cost-to-revenue calculations.

First, on OpenAI and Anthropic, two of the biggest AI players, which are both looking to launch their respective IPOs sometime soon.

As reported by Reuters, OpenAI is facing another year of heavy financial losses, with the company recently informing its investors that its projected 2026 revenue could be significantly lower than it had previously forecast.

As per Reuters: “OpenAI has told investors its annualized revenue for September was almost $50 billion, a drop ​from what it signaled earlier, a person familiar with the ‌matter told Reuters on Thursday. The company previously indicated to investors at a separate event that its revenue run rate for last month was approaching $70 billion.”

According to AI Funding Tracker, OpenAI has already weathered more than $20 billion in operating losses in the first half of 2026, with revenue of around $12 billion for the same period. The company had hoped to win more investor confidence ahead of a planned IPO with a better Q3 performance.

But a lower-than-expected revenue result could mean that OpenAI is on track to post a major financial loss in 2026.

Anthropic, meanwhile, recently shared that while its revenue is increasing, its costs are also set to skyrocket as the company spends big to keep up with the evolving AI development race.

As reported by CNBC, Anthropic reported a significant Q3 2026 revenue increase, rising almost 1,127% year over year to $17 billion. At the same time, the company also announced plans to spend more than $895 billion on compute and infrastructure over the next few years.

CNBC added that while Anthropic’s revenue is up 47% quarter over quarter, its compute and infrastructure costs increased by $14.4 billion for the nine months ended Sept. 30, up 329% compared to the same period last year.

Meanwhile, xAI owner Elon Musk told Grok Bot users on Nov. 1 that the company had implemented new restrictions on access because “the cost of serving Grok Bot was unsustainably high relative to revenue.”

Taken alongside these other notes, this is pretty much a summary of the entire AI industry, as companies pour more and more money into AI projects without a clear revenue pathway to justify that investment.

Meta is facing the same challenges. The company has seen strong early adoption of its Muse agents, which are its big hope for driving consumer AI adoption. However, Meta has also committed hundreds of billions of dollars to AI development and support infrastructure, which sets the bar very high for a cash flow-positive result.

Indeed, based on the company’s existing financial commitments to AI projects, it’s got a long, long way to go.

In 2025, Meta generated $200.97 billion, with $4.8 billion of that coming from non-advertising intake, which includes AI subscriptions.

In order to break even on its current AI spending commitments, Meta would need to generate around $100 billion per year from AI subscriptions and partnerships over the next decade.

In other words, Meta needs to build a whole new business offering that generates half as much income as one of the most profitable businesses in the world if it hopes to even come close to turning its AI investment into a profit anytime before 2036.

The operational cost-to-benefit ratio is almost too high to fathom. Moreover, with AI projects continuing to face opposition and negative perception increasing among consumers, many companies may be forced to reconsider their approaches and re-examine the openness of their AI models.

Restricting usage would reduce costs, as Musk said, but it could also slow adoption.

Then again, maybe taking generative AI tools out of the hands of the public, and reducing the amount of AI slop being published to social apps, would lead to a better outcome overall.

Doing that could also make it cost-prohibitive for scammers to generate AI replicas of other people’s content, which is an issue that Instagram Chief Adam Mosseri recently highlighted as a major concern.

Essentially, the cost crunch could soon see AI tools reducing their usage limits, which could lead to less AI use overall.

The question then is whether Meta will end up winning by eating the losses that other companies can’t in order to maximize the take-up of its Muse agents.

#7384. Amazon’s Ads nears $20 billion in Q3 as it bets on agentic AI to fuel further growth

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Amazon posted Q3 revenues of $201.9 billion today (Oct. 29), up 12% year-on-year, with its advertising offering generating almost $20 billion during the period, also up 12% during the period, making it one of the fastest-growing business units within the enterprise.

Those perusing the earnings report will note how revenue fell from $19.81 billion in Q2 to $19.64 billion in Q3, a decline of approximately 0.9%, although one caveat is that the sequential comparison is complicated by Prime Day occurring in Q2 this year rather than Q3.

While Amazon has not provided a specific dollar Q4/FY 2026 revenue forecast for its advertising services division — total revenue is expected to be in the range of $240 to $247 billion — it is expected to be a key pillar in its overall growth strategy.

“We expect year-over-year advertising revenue to grow in the high teens in fourth quarter 2026,” reads its official filing, which also trumpeted its recent launch of Amazon Ads Agent, a key growth engine for the division.

Intuitively, Amazon is putting AI at the center of its advertising business, previewing the consolidation of previously separate buying tools into a single platform that gives its technology greater responsibility for planning, executing, and optimizing campaigns — a concern for some.

Unveiled at the e-commerce giant’s flagship advertising conference, unBoxed, in San Francisco, California, last month, Amazon Ads Agent brings sponsored advertising, display, video, and audio into a unified buying experience.

The platform builds on Amazon’s earlier integration of its demand-side platform and advertising console, extending its conversational AI assistant across campaign creation, targeting, optimization, measurement, and customer support.

Advertisers can now use natural-language instructions to plan campaigns, generate creative, forecast audience reach, adjust budgets, and analyze performance. The system can also recommend and execute optimizations, reducing the manual work traditionally associated with programmatic advertising.

Delving deeper into Amazon’s publicity material, two offerings underpin the overhaul: Full-Funnel Campaigns and Digital Video Audio, or DVA+, as it’s more commonly termed.

One key aspect is Full-Funnel Campaigns, which combine sponsored ads, display, video, and streaming TV within a single campaign. Advertisers specify their products, creative and budget, while Amazon’s AI determines how campaigns run and continuously optimize toward objectives including new-to-brand customers and long-term sales.

The system also shares signals across upper- and lower-funnel advertising, including campaigns running on third-party open-internet inventory, with Amazon claiming beta testing shows up to 67% higher return on long-term ad spend and 29% lower cost per new-to-brand customer than manually configured full-funnel campaigns.

Meanwhile, DVA+ consolidates sponsored display, sponsored TV, and programmatic display, video, and audio buying. Advertisers can select an objective, budget and creative, leaving targeting and optimization to Amazon, or use advanced settings to retain conventional DSP controls.

For Amazon, the commercial opportunity extends beyond simplifying campaign management, as making programmatic buying accessible to smaller advertisers without specialist teams could expand adoption of its advertising infrastructure.

Meanwhile, integrating sponsored advertising with upper-funnel formats gives Amazon another opportunity to capture budgets traditionally managed through separate agency teams and buying platforms.

However, Amazon’s expansion comes amid renewed scrutiny of how large advertising platforms operate, a familiar debate in countless antitrust filings, and one underlined by recent charges against the e-commerce giant’s ad offering. In August, the Federal Trade Commission and 22 state attorneys general ⁠sued Amazon over alleged deceptive and unfair advertising auction practices.

The complaint alleges Amazon secretly introduced surcharges into auctions, resulting in Sponsored Products advertisers paying their own winning bids approximately 80% of the time by 2024, despite representations concerning second-price auctions. Amazon disputes the allegations, which remain unproven in court.

While the lawsuit concerns existing auction practices rather than the newly launched agentic platform, it highlights questions surrounding the transparency of automated advertising systems. Sources consulted by Digiday, who asked to remain anonymous to protect industry relationships, noted the maxim of “caveat emptor,” or, more simply, “buyer beware.” They cautioned:

Amazon maintains that automation does not necessarily require advertisers to surrender control. Speaking ahead of the formal unveiling of the new platform, Kelly MacLean, vp, Amazon demand-side platform, told Digiday that DVA+ advertisers retain authority over objectives, products, creative approval, budgets, pacing, flight dates, and location targeting.

Advanced settings also preserve controls over bidding, audience targeting, supply selection, and frequency. MacLean characterized that configuration as “human-led, AI-supported,” maintaining that buyers can retain the conventional DSP experience while using AI-assisted recommendations.

Amazon also says it provides reporting on where ads are delivered and is developing explanations for its agent’s recommendations. However, those explanations remain a work in progress, with MacLean acknowledging that Amazon is still determining how much information advertisers need to understand the rationale behind automated decisions.

Asked whether the new tooling would explain how individual Sponsored Ads auction prices were determined, including the role of reserve or calculated prices, Amazon did not commit to providing that information. Furthermore, company spokespeople maintained that the company “strongly disagrees with the FTC’s filing.”

Speaking separately with Digiday ahead of the recent unBoxed launch, Amit Bhattacharyya, Amazon Ads’ vp, Full-Funnel Products, AI & Modeling, acknowledged limitations in the controls available through Full-Funnel Campaigns.

For example, advertisers approve creative but cannot select individual channels or specific audiences. “We are figuring out how to give them more transparency on the audience side,” he told Digiday.

Bhattacharyya also noted that Amazon provides format-level reporting, while its long-term sales measurement uses shopping signals to predict sales over 12 months and then displays accumulated actual sales against those forecasts.

For Alex Brownstein, founder of AHB Advisors and a former McKinsey consultant, the challenge extends beyond whether Amazon provides reporting. “Transparency on an agent buy comes in five layers, and Amazon is more open on some than others,” he said.

Those layers encompass data, decisions, pricing, delivery and results. Brownstein also noted that DVA+ offers publisher-level reporting, while full-funnel campaigns provide format-level visibility without equivalent detail about channel pricing.

He also questioned how advertisers can independently validate outcomes when Amazon controls both the buying technology and much of the underlying purchase data. “Amazon is open where openness brings it more of the budget and closed where its advantage sits,” Brownstein said.

To mitigate any risk, Brownstein suggested advertisers use their own customer data and independent incrementality testing to assess whether Amazon’s reported performance translates into additional sales.

Meanwhile, Jay Mandel, co-founder of the Marketing Accountability Council, raised a related concern about the incentives governing automated buying decisions. “My concern is whose interests the AI agent serves when Amazon’s revenue goals and the advertiser’s business goals diverge,” he told Digiday.

Mandel argued that advertisers should be able to audit automated changes, inspect fees and inventory decisions, and challenge platform performance claims using independent measurement. He further opined, “If spending less with Amazon is better for the advertiser, will its agent recommend that?”

Amazon’s new platform offers advertisers a simpler route into programmatic buying while extending the company’s influence over how advertisers allocate budgets, and that’s a story that it’s selling to Wall Street.

However, its longer-term success will depend not only on whether automation improves performance, but also on whether advertisers can independently determine how those results were achieved, a story Madison Avenue will be more eager to hear.

Google’s Q3 earnings reveal AI Mode’s impact on search advertising growth, plus ongoing diminishing returns from ad tech.

At DMBS, agency leaders explained why retail media offers new opportunities to harness closed-loop measurement in a way that benefits both upper- and lower-funnel marketing efforts.

Digiday’s executive editor of news Seb Joseph and senior ad tech reporter Ronan Shields joined the Digiday Podcast to hash out ad tech’s Game of Thrones amid the server-side shift.

#7383. Meta delays commercial terms update indefinitely

The company’s upcoming commercial terms of service update has prompted regulators to seek more insight into the specific details.

Meta’s planned commercial terms of service update has become a significant topic of concern for the company, and it may now be forced to abandon the update altogether in order to avoid additional scrutiny.

Earlier this month, Meta began informing business users of an upcoming update to its commercial terms of service, which included a change in wording that could have allowed Meta to scrape the website of any business that maintained a Facebook or Instagram presence.

Legal experts raised the alarm, which prompted regulatory groups to pose questions about the change.

Meta has maintained that the purpose of the update is not related to data scraping, and is instead to help with data gathering for the sole purpose of ad targeting. Even so, in response to these queries, Meta delayed the launch of the updated terms, shifting the implementation date from Oct. 20 to Nov. 20.

Then last week, as reported by Reuters, Meta said that it could end up delaying the rollout indefinitely in order to address concerns raised by regulatory groups.

Among those is the UK’s Competition and Markets Authority, which, according to The Next Web, has opened an investigation into Meta’s commercial terms for businesses. The European Publishers Council is also assessing next steps amid rising concerns about the gathering of business data to power AI models.

It’s impossible to know the intention of Meta’s planned update, but it does seem plausible that the initial update was not intended to empower broad-scale data-scraping, and that Meta’s inclusion of the term “scraping,” which has a different context in the AI era, has caused this new issue.

But either way, Meta is now working to address concerns ahead of the planned update.

Meta has made a copy of its updated commercial terms available for review, with a note at the top of the screen still indicating that they will go into effect on Nov. 20.

#7382. Apple’s Safari block hands WPP a fresh case for InfoSum

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Apple’s crackdown on the tricks advertisers use to track the same person across different sites and devices keeps tearing through large parts of the ad industry. WPP (if its CEO is to be believed) has barely felt it.

Cindy Rose said as much on the company’s third-quarter earnings call today yesterday. Richard Kramer, founder of equities research firm Arete Research, asked how exposed WPP was. Minimally, according to Rose, because the holdco’s data business is no longer built around the practice Apple is now shutting down.

“What I will say is it doesn’t really impact our data solution at all because we don’t rely solely on identity matching,” Rose said on the call.

Apple sees it rather differently. In an Oct. 19 blog post titled “Blocking Identity Resolution Infrastructure,” the engineers behind Safari argued that the plumbing pitch misses the point. Each site may only be sharing its own customers’ details but the firm in the middle sees the same email arrive from all of them, which lets it connect a person’s activity across sites in a way no single site could. Apple also took issue with the idea that people agree to this, arguing that nobody signing up for a newsletter expects their address to end up with a third party or something else entirely.

Apple is hardly alone in that view. Since the General Data Protection Regulation arrived in 2018, regulators and platforms have been pulling the industry away from tracking individuals across the web, sometimes forcefully, often haltingly. Rose’s pitch is that WPP saw where that was headed.

“We bought InfoSum roughly two years ago. We’ve now fully integrated it into our WPP Open platform. It is a data collaboration platform that relies on a mix of data sources from consumer identity to our own WPP proprietary data to live signals from our ecosystem of data partners, nearly 400 of them,” said Rose. “We use all of this data to identify untapped growth audiences and predict consumer behavior and ultimately deliver growth outcomes for our clients. Therefore, I would say in the scheme of things, the impact on WPP to these iOS changes are minimal.”

Read that way, Rose was doing more than playing down a risk. She was making the case, again, for InfoSum.

It’s a case WPP has had to keep making. Plenty in the industry questioned the April 2025 deal and the doubts grew louder when Publicis agreed to pay $2.2 billion for LiveRamp this May. Publicis appeared to have bought the industry’s data switchboard. WPP appeared to have bought a niche. Five months on, LiveRamp is on Apple’s blocklist. WPP had already stopped using it by June.

The difference is where the matching happens. Apple’s block works in the browser, cutting Safari off from firms whose code collects email on a publisher’s page. InfoSum operates away from the browser. Each party’s data stays in its own sealed-off environment and in WPP’s newer setup inside the client or publisher’s own systems, with only the insights shared between them. That puts it outside the reach of what Safari is currently blocking.

“The subtext of course is that their deadly rival, Publicis, is dancing to a different tune,” said Charles Ping, managing director at M&A advisors Winterberry Group. “If Cindy can make the $2billion+ that Publicis spent on Liveramp (plus Lotame and Epsilon before that) by contrast look poor both strategically and financially on an earnings call then that’s a strategic investor message nailed.  And none of Publicis’ acquisitions have delivered European capability.”

None of this puts InfoSum entirely clear of Apple’s argument, of course. Consumer identity is still one of its inputs, as Rose noted, and connecting what different companies know about the same people is close to what Apple objected to. It just happens to with data the parties already hold, somewhere Apple’s browser doesn’t reach.

“In reality, this does not mean WPP is unscathed,” said Robert Webster, founder of AI marketing consultancy TAU and former WPP exec. “It also need signals to deliver on safari and those signals have been degraded from them as from everyone else. Many of the ad tech systems WPP uses are equally encumbered, if not more so by Apple’s policies — moreso because without the email match grids they are not able to bring tougher sufficiency login data to make up for Publicis’ well known advantage in match rate across email graphs.”

The comments came as WPP turnaround plan looks to be gathering momentum. Over the last quarter, its media arm’s revenue, the biggest for the holdco, returned to growth. WPP Media’s revenue excluding costs passed on to clients grew 0.3% on a like-for-like basis in the third quarter reversing a 2.8 decline in the previous quarter.

Getting there meant some tough calls along the way, none more closely watched than WPP’s decision to sit out the pitch for Coca-Cola’s North American media account while it fought to keep the roughly $2 billion Coke spends on media, data and tech everywhere else. On the call, Rose reasoned that pitching costs a lot of money and a lot of time, with no guarantee of a win at the end. That math would have carried extra weight for Rose because WPP had already lost the account to Publicis last year. Going back so soon after would have meant spending heavily to win back business Coke had chosen to move barely 18 months earlier.

“We didn’t participate in the North America pitch,” said Rose. “Mostly because we won the rest of the world, and we have a huge amount of work to do in a very short period of time. I had people, many, many, many people pitching for many, many, many weeks over the summer. I was with them myself in London and Shanghai and Mexico City.”

That said, it was not a decision that was easy.

“We made a tough call between spending yet another six weeks pitching for North America versus delivering against what we had already won,” said Rose. “It was a fine judgment call, which I made, and it is as simple as that, really. That is your question on Coca-Cola North America.”

#7378. From Celsius to Maybelline, more brands are lining up for the NYC Marathon’s 50th anniversary

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This story was first published by Digiday sibling Modern Retail.

This year’s New York City Marathon, which marks the 50th anniversary of the modern five-borough race and is expected to break participant records, is attracting brands to the world-famous race.

It’s no surprise that footwear brands like Nike, New Balance and Hoka are hosting their own pop-ups during the New York City Marathon weekend. But the brands showing up at the annual race represent a more diverse cross-section of retail than ever before, from beauty brands like Salt & Stone to restaurants around the city capitalizing on runners looking to carb-load. As the official and first-ever beauty sponsor of the marathon, Maybelline is also hosting a series of activations around the city, starting with a pre-race shakeout run in collaboration with running sunglasses brand Rendezvous Sports, featuring product giveaways and snacks.

As major sports events, like the U.S. Open and the Olympics, increasingly dominate consumer attention, more brands are finding ways to capitalize on sports moments. The New York City Marathon, specifically, also overlaps with running culture that has grown through run clubs. By leveraging strategic pop-ups, community events and targeted sponsorships, these companies are tapping into sports marketing opportunities to drive engagement and align themselves with the active lifestyles of today’s consumers.

Ahead of the New York City Marathon, energy drink brand Celsius is opening a Club Celsius pop-up in Brooklyn, a dedicated hub for a series of activations throughout the weekend. According to the company, the pop-up is meant to bring together runners, athletes and the New York public for the marathon. Located along the marathon route in Brooklyn, Club Celsius will host experiences ranging from pre-race prep to race-day cheering to post-race celebrations, and will be live from Oct. 30 to Nov. 2.

Attendees will get to customize race-day gear and sample Celsius drinks on site. The weekend kicks off Friday with a sunset group run led by DJ duo Sidequest and “Love Island” alum Corbin Mims, along with other fitness influencers.

The pop-up also builds on Celsius’ marketing efforts during last year’s New York City Marathon. In 2025, the company popped up with a similar concept called Energy House, where it hosted a dinner and panel discussion for athletes, and offered morning stretches, yoga and a recovery zone.

Kyle Watson, chief brand officer at Celsius, said the brand’s origin as a better-for-you energy drink for fitness enthusiasts makes the famous marathon a natural fit to sponsor.

“The brand was founded in fitness, and it remains an integral part of our DNA,” Watson said.

He added that the Club Celsius space “was created for the entire running community, and our programming reflects that,” from a shakeout run led by brand partners to a post-run live dating show and Medal Monday recovery session.

Watson said running continues to evolve beyond a fitness-only activity into a social gathering. “We wanted our activation to reflect the modernity of running culture and truly serve as a social destination for the weekend,” he said.

Mattress brand Saatva is hosting its first-ever New York City Marathon pop-up this year, as part of its ongoing effort to tap into sports marketing moments.

This week, Saatva hosted a wellness event at its Flatiron store, which included a panel on training and recovery featuring five-time NYC Marathon winner Tatyana McFadden and Frank Reneau, founder of A Mile. A Minute.

Ron Rudzin, founder and CEO of Saatva, told Modern Retail that the New York City Marathon’s growing global brand “reflects how much running has evolved beyond race day into a year-round culture centered on wellness, community and performance.”

As part of its marketing efforts around running, Saatva also has sponsorships as the official mattress and restorative sleep partner of USA Track & Field and Team USA at the 2028 Summer Olympics and Paralympic Games. Rudzin pointed to the Saatva marathon-week store event as the latest piece in this strategy to help put sleep and recovery at the center of the performance conversation.

This year also marks Vuori’s first-ever New York City Marathon activation.

The company is hosting weekend-long events at its Flatiron Manhattan store, including pre- and post-marathon live DJs, snacks and limited-edition items. On race day, the brand is hosting a pop-up along the marathon route in Williamsburg with a branded newsstand and cheer signs.

Ashley Kechter, global president of Vuori, said the company decided to celebrate the event by leaning into the city’s busy atmosphere throughout the weekend. “There’s a lot that makes the NYC Marathon special, but the energy and sense of connection around it are what stand out to us as a brand,” Kechter said.

Moreover, Kechter said Vuori’s roots in athletic wear make the marathon a fit for a pop-up. The company has already been marketing in this space by hosting its own run clubs over the past few years. “Bringing people together through movement and meaningful experiences is at the heart of Vuori,” Kechter said. “So we’re excited to do that on race day and beyond.”

#7377. Amid growing tension over workplace AI, agencies have a window to reimagine staffing

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This article is part of Digiday’s coverage of its Digiday Media Buying Summit. More from the series →

Marketing agencies across the ad industry have been enthusiastic adopters of AI agents. But their team structures, job descriptions and the career paths of their staff have been largely left untouched — even as the actual day-to-day work of media buyers and marketing execs has changed, and even as fluency with AI tools becomes a required skill.

“There is a baseline that everyone is expected to understand and be fluent in,” said Gila Wilensky, evp and head of data and media, North America, Monks, during a session at Digiday’s Media Buying Summit this week in Newport Beach, Calif.

At many agencies, fluency means using and developing AI agents to automate planning or media activation work. A Digiday survey published in May found that just 9% of media agencies were not pursuing agentic AI solutions of some kind.

That automation has saved time for individual workers, but few companies have successfully distributed AI gains across teams or departments. According to a recent estimate by Apollo Partners, while tech companies have improved average profit margins, and revenue-per-worker have risen among U.S. businesses since the introduction of generative AI in 2022, neither measure has improved among companies outside that sector.

That might change as the over-use of AI in corporate settings begins to make working life more tedious and more stressful — and brings tensions over individual usage of AI to the fore.

Agency attendees, speaking under Chatham House Rules at the Summit’s townhall session, said that colleagues both junior and senior are using tech as an all-purpose corner cutter — speeding up some processes but, in the same act, skipping over opportunities for critical thinking.

“I’ve been surprised by how much AI content I’m seeing come from vice presidents and C-suites,” said one attendee. “I don’t think they want to take the time.”

With that problem in mind — and the desire to capture and institutionalize AI productivity gains beyond experimental programs — some agencies are making space for staff education. At Monks, Wilensky said training of junior staff (and re-training of senior colleagues) held part of the answer.

“If we don’t provide that, things can go off the rails,” said Wilensky.

Nadja Bellan-White, group CEO of M&C Saatchi North America, said her agency had carved out an hour each week on employee timesheets that’s dedicated to training. “A learning agenda has to be operationalized throughout your organization,” she said. “You have to have a culture of always learning, always pushing forward.”

Monks was also beginning to review hiring for its early-career roles, Wilensky added. “We’re not hiring less for junior talent. But it’s [being] more strategic about who you hire and what tasks you want junior talent to do,” she said. “I don’t think that junior team members are going to be sitting [and] piecing spreadsheets and recording spreadsheets all day… that work is taken off of their plates.”

Meanwhile, access to tools is limited for some job roles, with developers or engineers getting access to more powerful (and more expensive) AI models — a measure principally designed to control compute costs, but which can serve to limit exposure to AI tools where they’re unhelpful.

Such measures represent early efforts to reconcile traditional ad agency staffing models with AI.

There are signs that AI is provoking some marketers to rethink their jobs. Freelance programmatic and strategy consultants have become avid users of the technology, while the Wall Street Journal recently reported a rise in companies employing marketing ‘engineers’, whose expertise was augmented by AI tooling.

Agencies, however, are only just now beginning to seriously consider how teams might be organised differently. That isn’t itself unusual — it took roughly forty years for factory owners to take advantage of the productivity gains offered by electric light.

Agencies won’t take as long as that to begin dialing in AI gains into team structures. Wilensky suggested that senior talent would be required to inject originality and a willingness to push the envelope into agency outputs.

“AI cannot take risks. That’s always going to be our job. AI is not creative. That’s always going to be our job. So no matter what level you are in your career, there’s opportunity to be strategic, to be creative, to take risks, to test,” she said.

Early career options, she added, may instead become focused on quality assurance or maintaining the fences agencies build to prevent AI agents from going rogue.

“We don’t have this role today, but there will be a fulltime job of AI checkers. That’s my premonition,” said Wilensky. “That’s going to be a career that our kids will do when they are older – there’ll be AI checkers in the same way that people in journalism used to be like fact checkers.”

#7374. After AI backlash, Svedka challenges consumers to create Super Bowl ad

Let Marketing Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The Sazerac-owned vodka brand is pivoting after using artificial intelligence to produce its 2026 big game debut.

Svedka is handing over the reins for its next Super Bowl ad to consumers, per a press release. The Sazerac-owned brand is challenging consumers to make a 15-second commercial featuring its ready-to-drink Vodka Water cocktail offering, which launched this summer and is sold in a clear can — what the brand claims is a category-first innovation.

Consumers can submit ads to a microsite until Dec. 1, with the winner receiving a $500,000 prize. The contest comes after the vodka brand faced controversy earlier this year for using artificial intelligence to bring its fembot mascot to life as part of its first Super Bowl ad. Despite the backlash, Sveda has been the top volume growth driver in U.S. spirits in the last year, per Nielsen data cited by the brand.

“We're proud of what we accomplished in 2026, but we heard plenty of people say they could do a better job. And we welcomed the debate,” Sara Saunders, group president and CMO at Sazerac, said in a statement. “We can't wait to see how fans bring the brand's newest innovation to life.”

Svedka is making a digital tool kit available to consumers as they create ads that can be “AI-led, hilariously low-budget, unexpectedly cinematic or creatively ambitious,” per press details.

To launch the contest, Svedka teamed with Andy Cohen for an interview with the Svedbot appearing on YouTube in which the reality TV host notes that the previous ad was called “bold, bizarre and a dystopian nightmare.” Cohen will also help to evaluate the submissions.

Svedka’s 30-second national ad that ran during Super Bowl LX was managed by the brand’s in-house team with production by AI innovation lab Silverside. The spot represented the first national Super Bowl commercial dedicated solely to vodka in over three decades, and featured a brand mascot that had recently returned to advertising after a 12-year hiatus.

That campaign also included a call for user-generated content, with Svedka partnering with actress Victoria Justice to encourage consumers to help choreograph the ad via dance moves posted on Instagram or TikTok.

#7373. Media Buying Briefing: Overheard at Media Buying Summit: How AI is helping and hurting client relations and office work

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This article is part of Digiday’s coverage of its Digiday Media Buying Summit. More from the series →

Despite the headlong rush by virtually everyone in marketing and media to adopt AI tools and technology — from sophisticated agentic planning and measurement aids to things as ordinary as using an LLM to help write emails — the agency side at least finds itself leaning over its proverbial skis just a bit.

That’s one of the biggest takeaways from the two townhall sessions at last week’s fall Media Buying Summit in Newport Beach, Calif. As you know, the townhalls are held under Chatham House rules, which offer anonymity in exchange for candor when the agency-only group met to discuss their biggest challenges (and some solutions) in their jobs.

The following are direct quotes from the attendees, but have been lightly edited for space and clarity.

“We see a lot of our clients with thinner budgets, but expecting the world. And we go over hours quite a bit because they just ask for more things than are in the plan. We have to pull back sometimes and level set, but it still gets hard when the clients just expect so much.”

“It comes down to the brief. I’ve seen some of the worst briefs I’ve ever seen in my career recently, and it’s because they’re so disjointed and there’s no through line on what the not only the marketing goal is as well as the business goal … I think AI is making it worse. It’s like pontificating on a sentence structure that makes it 10 pages long instead of a sentence. And less is more when it comes to a brief. In that case, especially when you’re trying to drive down to your goals.”

“Yeah, briefs have definitely gotten worse, and they’re also like jam packed with a lot of things that feel like they start to blur together the swim lanes between what media’s job is versus a creative job or a PR driven job.”

“We’ve had living briefs of sorts. You fill out the questions first, and we have the follow up meeting, and we go through it to make sure we really understand what you think you’re putting in there. A lot of folks have those meetings after the fact, but if they are being bad clients and they’re doing things that they shouldn’t, then maybe it’s a way to circumvent that, get ahead of it before you get this word vomit brief that doesn’t mean anything.”

“We’ve just written the briefs for the clients … they give us their things and we say we’re just going to write this for you, and we write it. They’re like, ‘This is great,’ And our team also thinks this is great because our team just wrote it.”

“I’ve actually been surprised by how much AI content [there is at] a higher strategy level … places like the VPs and the C-suite. I know they know how to do these things. I don’t think they want to take the time to do it. Because this is much easier.”

“I’ve been getting more and more that even with our higher ups at the agency themselves because of that same mentality.” But we’re seeing the same thing from clients too that give us a generated prompt of what they think that they need.”

“I’ve been in meetings where I can tell every person who contributed to that meeting, it’s all AI generated slides where I’m like, ‘You’ve provided the bare minimum information and it just spit these out — you didn’t even look at them. You just threw them up there.’ It kind of has that uncanny valley feeling — they think this makes sense, but it doesn’t make sense. It’s very prevalent, probably more than we actually realize.”

“If they use it to write a quick email or to organize their thoughts, I don’t see any shame in that. But I think there’s probably a deeper level of generation happening that is leading to this output where this is a mess. This is not clear. This doesn’t feel human.”

“We actually had a client who had somebody else write a brief for them, and it was clearly written with AI. And you could tell the client didn’t have any idea what was in it because we went back to the client [and said] ‘Half of this stuff is not doable.’ It was 18 pages long. It was it was a lot.”

“If you’ve got a super long email with 50 m dashes in it, maybe call the person back and ask what they want to do, versus responding to it and having your team run around for a week trying to write the right response with 60 m dashes back. I think we should do a better job as agency leaders to train our staff to interact with the clients in a way that is personal versus just responding to back and forth emails.”

“There’s two forks in the road for the world of agencies. One fork is, ‘I’m going to fully automate everything and eliminate as many humans and costs as possible.’ The other fork in the road is, ‘I’m going to create efficiency and enable the humans I already have, and lean into expertise and make those experts more efficient at what they do, so they can deliver more value for clients.’ So, if I was working at an organization that’s going for full automation and limiting humans, I would be scared. I would argue that’s not the right strategy.”

“I tell our staff, ‘Do your resume every six months, and if you’re not learning stuff here, advancing your skill sets, you should quit and go work someplace else.’ Because you have these people that have been at agencies for 10 or 15 or 20 years. That is not the way to evolve your business and or help your clients grow. So if we’re not pushing the staff to learn new things and keep the sort of ship afloat, you’re going to have these issues where an AI bot spends $10 million versus $10,000 in a DSP, and it’s going to be like, I don’t know what to do.”

Few topics are as deeply divisive as principal media. Which is why Digiday used it to launch a new franchise at the Media Buying Summit last week: The Case For and Against.

Stagwell’s purchase of Freestar marks an expansion of the agency’s owned media practice — and provides a decent jumping off point to rehearse some of the pro and anti arguments around agencies deploying client ad spend agains owned, principal or proprietary media.

Onstage at the summit, Forrester vp and senior agency analyst Jay Pattisall argued for the practice’s use, against Crossmedia co-founder and CEO Kamran Asghar, who took the opposing view.

Pattisall said that principal media was an established way of doing business for large agencies, one that could be handled fairly in the client’s interests, provided agencies disclosed its use and provided opt-outs for skeptical clients.

“It’s here, it’s not going anywhere… so if you’re going to do it, do it well,” he said. Moreover, Pattisall said principal media could provide a release valve for agencies suffering margin compression, publishers struggling to evenly monetize inventory, and clients looking for flexibility in their media investments. “You’re getting a win-win-win,” he said.

Asghar, in contrast, said principal based media trading eroded marketing services groups’ roles as agents — and it barred agencies from meeting demand from marketers for a pricing model or relationship that rewards meeting business outcomes.

“We’ll never get to outcome-based pricing in our industry as long as principal media exists, because a revenue model of taking a kickback or a volume bonus far outweighs anything you’re going to ever earn on an outcome-based basis,” he said.

Asghar argued that if agencies are to engage in principal-based media, they should take a leaf from the financial sector’s book: separate trading and advisory  businesses properly, so that clients opting out of principal media can ensure they’re dealing with an honest broker. “There needs to be clear separation between church and state, in my mind,” he said.

“Where there’s murkiness, there’s money to be made,” he added. — Sam Bradley

Whether it’s avid fandom or long-held nostalgia, viewers with an emotional connection to a movie or a streaming series make for a fertile advertising audience, according to a report from Disney’s U.K. arm. The House of Mouse surveyed 1,100 streaming users and measured their engagement levels in an eye-tracking study, as well as a brand lift study. The bottom line? Fandom means a hyper-engaged audience:

“I think what’s driving changes in marketing expenditure is not a bad economy. It’s a good economy. I think change is being driven by technological revolution, not not by economic devolution … in our segment growth, we’re up 23% in digital transformation. We’re up 100% in the marketing cloud. If you project out growth in those two segments for the next two years, we become a fundamentally revalued company, more of a tech and tech services company.”

Google’s Q3 earnings reveal AI Mode’s impact on search advertising growth, plus ongoing diminishing returns from ad tech.

At DMBS, agency leaders explained why retail media offers new opportunities to harness closed-loop measurement in a way that benefits both upper- and lower-funnel marketing efforts.

Digiday’s executive editor of news Seb Joseph and senior ad tech reporter Ronan Shields joined the Digiday Podcast to hash out ad tech’s Game of Thrones amid the server-side shift.

#7369. Amazon says more than 400M customers have used Alexa for Shopping in the last 12 months

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This story was first published by Digiday sibling Modern Retail.

During its fiscal third-quarter earnings call on Thursday, Amazon shed more light on how its conversational AI shopping assistant, Alexa for Shopping, is powering more transactions.

More than 400 million customers have now used Alexa for Shopping in the last 12 months, up from 350 million at the end of July. Meanwhile, customers who use Alexa for Shopping are spending 50% more than those who don’t, Amazon CEO Andy Jassy said during the company’s earnings call on last Thursday.

The figures represent how AI shopping has become a powerful growth lever for Amazon and a way to nudge shoppers toward more sales, especially in a time when the business has matured and growth in other areas is harder to come by.

On Thursday, Amazon reported that net sales for the third quarter came in at $201.9 billion, up 12% from last year. The bulk of that came from Amazon Web Services, or AWS, which reported segment sales up 39% year over year.

Growth in many of Amazon’s e-commerce-related segments slowed during the quarter. However, much of that was because Prime Day was held at the end of June this year, rather than in mid-July. As a result, Amazon recorded the sales growth from Prime Day in the second quarter; last year, it was recorded in the third quarter.

Online Stores revenue was up 2% year over year. Last year, during the third quarter, Amazon reported that online Stores revenue grew 10% between the third quarter of 2024 and 2025.

Similarly, third-party seller services and advertising services grew 12% and 24%, respectively, between the third quarter of 2024 and the third quarter of 2025. By contrast, between the third quarter of 2025 and the third quarter of 2026, they grew 9% and 11%, respectively.

The public-facing history of Alexa for Shopping goes back three years. In February 2024, Amazon released an AI shopping assistant that was then known as Rufus. In May of this year, Amazon rebranded Rufus to Alexa for Shopping, choosing to adopt the name of its conversational voice assistant many shoppers were already used to.

Since then, Amazon has made Alexa for Shopping more visible on its website and app, and added new capabilities.

Amazon has made headlines for its walled-garden approach to AI, as it has blocked platforms like ChatGPT and Meta’s Muse shopping agent from scraping its site.

Jassy hinted during Thursday’s earnings call that that may eventually change — which he has said before — but ultimately, he said, the company wants to push as many customers to Alexa for Shopping as possible.

“Though we anticipate working with horizontal agents, we believe Alexa for Shopping is going to be the primary shopping agent customers use to shop Amazon,” Jassy said.

Google search was the line item buyers never questioned. That’s changing.
