#7367. Where agency strategists see opportunity as AI challenges their worth

Let Marketing Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

While strategists fear the tech will replace their roles, the majority agree that there are also reasons to be optimistic, according to a WARC report.

The advertising industry has faced significant challenges this year, marked by agency consolidations at major holding companies and sweeping headcount reductions. Between August 2025 and August 2026, the industry recorded a loss of 6,900 jobs, per Emarketer.

While there are many factors behind the cuts, one of the most prominent has been the rise of artificial intelligence across all aspects of the industry. From data analysis to campaign generation, AI’s adoption has been swift across nearly all marketing disciplines, leaving many executives apprehensive about the future.

Still, 66% of agency-side strategists agree there are clear reasons to be optimistic, according to WARC’s Future of Strategy 2026 report.

“We’re not saying the problems are solved, but we are saying the tides may be turning,” said Lena Roland, content director at WARC Strategy. “After a period of layoffs and consolidation, there’s a sense that things might be stabilizing and strategists can [begin] to look forward again.”

The reasons for the guarded positivity are simple: Clients are seeing a need for thinking that is rooted in human insights, Roland explained. Strategy influence with clients jumped to 50%, up from 41% in 2025, per WARC.

“This indicates that clients are increasingly recognizing the value of strategy, particularly at a time when growth is harder to achieve,” Roland said. “AI can help us understand the world as it is; strategists can help us understand what it can become.”

Ironically, AI is helping fuel strategists’ confidence. Nearly two-thirds, 65%, of the 489 agency-side strategists surveyed said AI gives them time for more nuanced, strategic work. Simultaneously, 55% believe the tech will refocus strategy on human perspectives. WARC’s report was fielded in August.

“Strategists are positioned as the best people to be guardians of reality by grounding ideas in real human insights,” Roland said. “In an age of AI, humanity is a competitive advantage. AI can’t replace human judgment or intuition, and it can’t ask the questions that are really worth asking.”

Not yet, anyway. Despite their optimism for the near-term, strategists are more wary about the long-term. More than half of the strategists polled in WARC’s study — 56%, up from 41% last year — are concerned that AI will eventually advance enough to replace them.

“They’re more optimistic about the future, but they’re also realistic about the scale of change,” Roland said. “The strategists that will thrive are those who learn to use AI well, while still bringing the human perspective, critical thinking and commercial understanding that makes their work valuable.”

However, the technology could become a double-edged sword: The same tools that strategists are using to move upstream with clients could also be leveraged by account executives, explained Michael Farmer, chairman and CEO at marketing consultancy Farmer & Company.

“When agencies talk about AI [right now], they’re not talking about how they can do a more strategic job for their clients,” Farmer said. “If they have the will, the senior account executives can [use AI] to offer more strategy.”

Doing so would elevate account executives to more of a business partner role, rather than one more akin to a traffic manager whose job is to get stuff out the door, Farmer said. He explained that over the past 30-some years, the account executive role has been diminished through the rise of programmatic negotiating, the unbundling of media and creative and the introduction of strategists in the late ‘90s and early 2000s.

“They took away a responsibility that the account people already had,” Farmer said. “They used to tell me when I first got started that the strategist was ‘the conscience of the consumer’ … They were there to kind of provide discipline between caring about the brand and caring about the brief that they got from the client.”

Often, that meant rewriting the briefs accordingly, Farmer said. Now, AI can often write those briefs as well — if not better — than a strategist can, which is reflected in the sentiment from strategists that they have more time to think beyond the brief. Agencies and their strategists will need to find how to prove their value beyond their traditional remit.

“Strategy as a discipline has too often been treated as a supporting function, or even thrown in for free, rather than recognized as a valuable service in its own right,” Roland said. “There’s an opportunity now for strategists to become genuine growth partners, helping shape not just how a brand communicates, but where the business goes next.”

Fortunately for strategists, many are already aware of the need to prove business results. In WARC’s survey, 80% of strategists said they must develop stronger evidence to prove their business impact, and 57% said they need a new value proposition and compensation model.

“AI is a great opportunity, but you have to be a strategist to realize that it could help you tap that unrealized potential,” Farmer said.

Which is ultimately why strategists see hope in the face of AI’s headwinds. Businesses will always be under pressure to demonstrate growth and will always need people who can find new ways to achieve it, whether that is on their own or with the help of AI, Roland explained.

“Businesses will always need people who can diagnose and solve problems,” Roland said. “The best strategists will use AI to get beyond the obvious and connect creativity to commercial outcomes, helping businesses see possibilities they couldn’t see before.”

Editor’s note: Informa, which owns a controlling stake in Marketing Dive’s publisher Informa TechTarget, is also invested in WARC. Informa has no influence over Marketing Dive’s coverage.

#7364. Google search loses its free pass with buyers

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

For the better part of two decades, Google search was the line item media buyers rarely had to defend. AI Overviews complicated that. Google now answers more queries on the results page itself, which changes how often people click through to an advertiser and what each click is worth.

That, in turn, means fewer prime ad slots around the search results people see, and Google’s automated buying tools, now the default for most campaigns, are quick to pay up for the ones that remain. Q4 makes it worse as seasonal advertisers bid up CPCs in the quarter that matters most.

Nearly a month into that quarter, advertisers want to know what all that extra spend is buying. Across conversations with 18 media buyers, Digiday found clients putting Google through the kind of scrutiny usually saved for newer channels.

“We’re having to look much more closely at what happens after the click and whether those clicks are actually producing qualified leads and revenue,” said Lindsay Marty, founder of Above the Bar Marketing.

That’s left her clients increasingly open to shifting a portion of their Google search budgets into channels including Meta and Local Services Ads if performance weakens. Marty added: “The amount being questioned is generally around 10% to 20% of the Google budget when that conversation comes up.”

Google’s own numbers point the same way. Search ad revenue grew 12% in the third quarter, Alphabet’s slowest pace this year, and executives blamed part of the slowdown on AI Mode — Google’s chatbot-style search tab.

The numbers are part of a bigger shift in how people use the internet. More of them are handing the search, and sometimes the purchase itself, to AI agents, and the money underneath the web is starting to follow. Where it lands is anyone’s guess. Advertisers and their agencies are having to place their bets now.

Take Bryce Hamlin, vp of data intelligence at Power Digital. He said media mix modelling and geo-testing were showing a greater impact from upper-funnel media and less from search. Dollars tend to follow whatever the models credit.

“Google search ads are still very powerful, but in the next two-to-three years, we will see a lot of searches happen off of Google because of LLMs,” he said. “We’re already seeing the beginning of this trend.”

Hamlin said the growing use of marketing science, AI and incremental measurement is also prompting advertisers to question whether some of their Google search budget would work harder elsewhere.

“An increasing number of marketers are asking, ‘is a portion of my budget better spent elsewhere’ — to the tune of 10% to 30% of the Google search budget getting reallocated,” he added.

Accuracast’s group CEO Farhad Divecha said the percentage within the Google budgets being questioned by his team’s clients is about 10% for now, but given the dynamic and competitive nature of Black Friday and  Cyber Monday, this could change to as much as 20% to 30% quite rapidly.

The size of those budgets vary, though search remains one of the biggest lines in most media plans. It took $114.2 billion of U.S. digital ad spending last year, or 38.8% of the total, according to the Interactive Advertising Bureau and PricewaterhouseCoopers. For mid-sized and larger advertisers that employ agencies, a Google search budget can run from tens of thousands of dollars a month into the millions.

Branded keyword CPCs rose 5% in the third quarter, roughly in line with non-branded terms, according to Michelle Merklin, vp of search at Tinuiti.

“CPCs keep rising,” she continued, though she’s keeping a closer eye on how much search there is to buy. Impressions and clicks climbed over the same period, which leaves advertisers paying a bit more for a bigger pool of demand.

Some buyers have done the math on that trade and don’t like the answer.

“It’s not just traffic, but also the ROI from Google that’s been squeezed in recent years, making it less attractive as a channel,” said Divecha. “We definitely see advertisers looking at other options to invest budgets in, especially ChatGPT.”

He said Accuracast’s clients are looking at alternatives and could move money away from Google if click volumes began to dip. Some execs are finding that channels outside search play a greater role in driving conversions than traditional attribution models suggest. The agency’s AI-led campaign management platform, Unyte.AI, for example, automatically reallocates budget away from Google when returns weaken, shifting spend to other platforms delivering incremental conversions at a better return on investment, Divecha explained.

More of that money is landing on Meta and TikTok, where advertisers are trying to build awareness and consideration earlier in the funnel. According to Merklin, some advertisers were reallocating budgets from less efficient parts of search, including campaigns targeting competitors’ keywords or “conquesting.” But she said most advertisers managed by Tinuiti were seeking incremental budget or reallocating spend from elsewhere, rather than cutting search to fund upper-funnel activity; the trend contributed to a 16% rise in Facebook ad spend among Tinuiti’s U.S. clients in the third quarter.

“You have to create the demand before it can be captured,” she explained. “There’s been a shift recently into the mid and upper funnel in order to produce more brand search volume.”

That shift doesn’t necessarily mean advertisers are abandoning search altogether. Some see paid search as increasingly important as organic traffic comes under pressure.

“It’s only [becoming] more important to be in that paid space because organic search traffic is being impacted,” said Casey Sheehan, vp, group director at RPA.

That said, fewer buyers treat it as the bedrock of a media plan.

“What’s coming up more in client conversations is the need to be present earlier in the discovery journey,” said Mark Byrne, director of paid media at Brave Bison. “Google search remains strong at capturing existing demand, while paid social and YouTube help build awareness and consideration before someone actively searches.”

It’s a rethink that reaches all the way down to the search results page, where paid and organic listings now get judged together. Byrne said advertisers were looking more closely at where they needed to bid to capture valuable traffic, and where they could pull back, weighing the combined impact on revenue, return on ad spend and cost per acquisition.

“From our perspective the most effective strategy is to have a really strong presence in your organic AI citations, and then also a strong paid search presence,” said Wpromote’s head of paid search Valerie Dorer.

On that basis, what’s happening to search ad spending is arguably to be expected as Google’s ads business adjusts around however people are going to access and share information moving forward. Indeed, ads in AI Mode are already expanding to new markets in Brazil, Mexico, and the United Kingdom while it is also scaling the ads already present in markets like Australia, Canada, India and Kenya.

Victor Batista, global search lead at Jellyfish, put a finer point on it. He said clients were not reducing their overall Google spend “in a targeted manner”. Instead, the “Google ecosystem is expanding its footprint in discussions as the AI experience is enhanced and more widely understood,” he added.

Clients are, in other words, identifying opportunities to shift spending from traditional keyword-based search campaigns to AI-powered alternatives such as AI Max and Performance Max, while also increasing investment in YouTube and Demand Gen. Overall, Batista said, budget diversification remained “generally contained within the Google ecosystem.”

Google remains a major channel for capturing existing demand, particularly when consumers are actively searching for products or services. But as search behavior changes and advertisers take a closer look at which channels are driving incremental returns, Google is facing greater scrutiny over its place in clients’ media plans.

“The incremental 10% to 20% is becoming less automatic,” said Marty. “Advertisers increasingly want Google to prove that spend is still the best use of those dollars.”

In response to this article, a Google spokesperson said: “Every day, millions of businesses choose Search Ads to drive growth. AI Overviews create new opportunities for brands to be discovered and help shoppers make more confident decisions. As Search becomes more conversational, our AI-powered campaigns help advertisers capture these new opportunities, while keeping them firmly in control.”

Customers who use Alexa for Shopping are spending 50% more than those who don’t.

AI agents are becoming common among agencies, but most companies haven’t grasped their potential to reshape teams.

The brands showing up at the annual race represent a more diverse cross-section of retail than ever before.

#7359. Giggly Squad’s SiriusXM deal signals a shift from podcast networks to creator ecosystems

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Last month, SiriusXM signed a multi-year deal with creators Paige DeSorbo and Hannah Berner for their Giggly Squad podcast reportedly valued at $160 million. Weeks later, the duo sold out four different nights for a live podcast tour at Madison Square Garden. That unprecedented deal signals a broader shift in podcasting: platforms are competing for creator-led businesses, not just shows.

“It’s even caught us on our heels, honestly, the demand for Giggly,” said Kelli Hurley, vp and head of content and podcast partnership at SiriusXM. “We were prepared, we were all hands on deck, but it has been just unprecedented in terms of what we’re seeing from the marketplace.”

Tom Webster, partner at trade association Sounds Profitable, said that despite podcast reach remaining flat since 2024, 8% of people who have consumed a podcast say they started in just the last 30 days. That’s a steady influx of new customers, and platforms like Spotify, Apple, and SiriusXM are trying to court them.

SiriusXM svp of podcast content Adam Sachs started working in podcasting 15 years ago, and he’s had a bird’s-eye view of the shifting landscape.

“The power of the network was what mattered [back then]. We would have these horizontal networks with a bunch of different shows in the network that all shared a sensibility,” he explained. “Now the approach is way more verticalized. We’re working with specific talent as opposed to a specific network, and helping them build a vertical ecosystem where their fans can engage. It’s sort of flipped.”

Building those vertical ecosystems can involve simple, expected things like helping a creator with their AV setup to far-reaching, off-platform support that includes IRL events.

“When we’re making these deals, we are increasingly thinking about a podcast as audio, video, social, and live. It’s those four legs of the stool, and we want to be a part of all of it,” said Sachs.

SiriusXM isn’t the only platform looking to work with creators beyond just hosting their show. Bryan Barletta, partner at Sounds Profitable and president of Podcast Movement, said Amazon’s investment in the NFL brother duo podcast, New Heights, signals a major shift in the partnership dynamic.

“We’re entering a new phase of podcasting where a distinct upper tier of creators is emerging, with significant separation from those coming up behind them,” he said.

The top tier of podcasting is drawing live audiences at arena scale. Giggly Squad is the first female-hosted podcast to headline Madison Square Garden, and tickets that averaged in the mid-$100s sold out fast. The sheer scope of the Giggly deal led Sirius to take a step back, according to Hurley. “In the past couple of weeks we took a total pause…we’ve got to do a full reset in how we want to go to market with this asset,” she said.

And brands are increasingly looking to get involved at multiple points in a creator podcast’s presence, from branded equipment to distribution support to showing up at IRL events. Now, more brands are heading into partnership talks wanting to “buy a creator,” according to Hurley. They’re asking for exclusivity, but Sirius wants to give them more.

“[We want] to be a one-stop shop,” she said.

Other platforms are offering a variety of ways for creators to connect with brands. Substack recently launched early access signups for dynamic ad insertion and plans for creators to leverage programmatic sponsorships in early 2027. Beehiiv just launched similar tools, dropping dynamic ad insertion, campaign management, and programmatic access just this past week.

“It gives creators more flexibility: run their own sponsor campaigns, put current ads into older episodes, and open unsold spots to programmatic demand when there’s a match,” said Ainsley Rossitto, head of podcast at beehiiv.

Since the mid-2020s, podcasts have aggressively pivoted to video. Sounds Profitable reports that now, only 17% of Americans expect a podcast to be audio-only. Substack’s head of video, Zach Taylor, told Digiday that half of their 350 highest revenue creators were using both audio in video in April 2024. “By February 2025, that number had grown to 82%,” he said.

The inclusion of a video component for podcasts makes it easier for discovery, as show clips can circulate on other social media platforms like YouTube, TikTok, and Instagram.

“Your podcast doesn’t have to be on video, but you have to have video,” said Barletta. Sounds Profitable’s research showed 84% of podcast clip consumers say clips led them to become regular podcast listeners at least sometimes.

Sachs said SiriusXM is seeing algorithmic growth through YouTube because of its built-in discoverability.

There are new pivots on the horizon: short-form content and a return to traditional radio. Sachs said Sirius is looking to do more with creators who are short-form-only content (monetizing their content, empowering them to do live events, and maybe even getting them on the radio), and are also supporting podcasters like Giggly Squad in creating their own radio shows — all in service of grabbing bigger slices of that audience pie.

“The radio audience is very distinct from the podcast audience; it’s a really disparate audience in terms of demographics, so we don’t see that as cannibalization at all. We see it as incremental reach,” Sachs said. “Giggly Squad is going to have a significant radio component, as well.”

Publishers are pushing back against Meta’s AI scraping terms, testing their leverage over a platform they still rely on.

Political and news creators are relied on now more than ever, but what platforms are working for them? Which ones aren’t?

#7355. People Inc. is building custom APIs to make its journalism easier for AI agents to use

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

People Inc. is moving toward custom APIs to help AI agents access its content more efficiently.

Rather than making AI systems shift through full webpages, People Inc., is giving them the article text and key details in a simpler format, according to Jon Roberts, People Inc.’s chief innovation officer.

“If you are an agent and you’re reading an HTML page, 90% of the content is junk meant for a browser to read, not an agent,” Roberts told delegates at Digiday’s Media Strategies London event. “It will really distract the agent, and it will be incredibly expensive to process all these tokens that it doesn’t need.”

The custom APIs strip out that extra material, sending the article text in Markdown along with details such as who wrote it and when it was published or updated and other “trust signals that matter.” The move comes as People Inc. shifts its broader investment priorities: the company laid off about 6% of its workforce last week. The cuts underscore the pressure on publishers to find durable businesses as AI reshapes how audiences discover and access journalism.

People Inc. first gave partners access to its content through web crawling, then through Amazon S3 buckets, which let the company send updates and retract content. It is now moving toward custom APIs, Roberts said, because crawling is an inefficient way for AI systems to get information.

Strategically, that could make access more efficient and give People Inc. greater control over how its journalism is delivered to AI partners.

“Every token that needs to be processed is expensive,” said Roberts. “If you throw it the entire HTML header, that’s abuse. Right, that’s not reasonable.”

AI companies are beginning to recognize the importance of knowing when publishers created or updated content, Roberts said. People Inc. is building those details into its content for its own AI tools, then using what it learns to offer AI partners a more useful way to access that material. The publisher is not focused on advertising directly to AI agents, he added.

The urgency comes as traffic from AI agents and agentic browsers grew 7,851% year over year, according to HUMAN’s 2026 State of AI Traffic report.

The challenge is already pressing for publishers trying to distinguish useful, customer-led agents from bots scraping content. “We need to act quickly on it because it’s not going to be something in the future — it’s happening now,” Daily Mail director of SEO and editorial commerce Carly Steven said at Digiday’s Media Strategies London event. “So that is a fairly urgent need that we all need to address and get our heads around,” she added.

Publishers are testing different ways to adapt their content and services for AI systems. People Inc. is moving toward structured access for AI partners. The Economist is experimenting with making marketing and B2B sales material outside of its paywall more easily readable for agents. Time has also converted its pages from HTML to Markdown and has even started to sell ads to those agents visiting its site.

Alongside figuring out which automated requests to allow, publishers are experimenting with how to package their journalism for AI systems. Forbes is also testing how to make some content more usable by AI agents. For its Vetted product reviews, the publisher has created Markdown versions of its content and an “MCP layer” while exploring separate presentations for booths and human readers.

Nina Gould, Forbes’ chief innovation officer, said the approach could help the publisher reach people using AI tools, but emphasized that the strategy is still developing a planned ChatGPT product-recommendation plugin that has not yet gone live. “On something like ads for agents, we’re still working on that,” she added.

#7351. How political creators are juggling platforms to find the three Rs: reach, revenue and rules

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Political creators need platforms that can deliver reach, revenue and clear rules. But no single platform reliably provides all three: YouTube offers ad revenue, Instagram can deliver huge audiences without paying creators and TikTok’s moderation and engagement can be unpredictable.

“A good platform for a political creator does four things,” said Ryan Schram, executive director of the Center for the Creator Economy at Syracuse University. “It helps new audiences find your work, it pays you fairly for that work, it tells you clearly what the rules are, and it lets you keep your relationship with your audience if you leave. Most platforms do one or two of those well. Very few do all four.”

Schram said that political creators gravitate to where political conversations are already happening, noting a 2024 PEW Research study that found that 85 percent of the most-followed news influencers are on X, half are on Instagram, and 44 percent are on YouTube.

“Journalists, campaigns, and elected officials are on X, and its users come for news. The catch is that creators and audiences aren’t in the same places,” Scharm said. “Audiences are larger on YouTube and Facebook, so the most successful political creators publish on several platforms rather than relying on one.”

Ahead of particularly contentious U.S. midterm elections, influencers are shaping narratives and highlighting key issues. Digiday spoke with six political creators and Schram about which platforms they are finding most fruitful, and which they’re struggling with.

YouTube remains a mainstay for many news and politics creators. “YouTube has the largest news audience and pays the most,” said Scharm. “It’s paid out more than $100 billion over four years and shares 55% of ad revenue.”

As far as the algorithm that drives that revenue, a YouTube spokesperson told Digiday it does “not favor or penalize content based on political orientation.” There are instances where YouTube’s systems “elevate high-quality voices,” like when major breaking news happens and the platform gets a special “breaking news shelf” on the homepage.

YouTube has leaned into news creators over the last few years, with its news, civics and podcast partnerships program, led by Brendan Feldman, offering advice on how to grow and monetize their channels. “For these last few years, a lot of focus has been on the real renaissance of journalists coming to YouTube, many in an independent capacity…and how we can best support them in building really vibrant businesses on our platform, connecting with audiences,” said Feldman.

Creator Aaron Parnas said he focuses on YouTube and Facebook in terms of monetization, though it’s only about 1 percent of his revenue pie.

“YouTube is the platform most worth investing in and also the hardest to grow on,” said V Spehar of Under the Desk News. “But if someone was just starting, I’d say focus on YouTube. It’s the most stable and fairly monetizes – but it’s hard to grow. So you may have to create for YouTube first, and then use clips to build discoverability by posting the clips on all socials until you figure out which social platform you perform best on.”

Several of the creators Digiday spoke with said Instagram has good engagement, but at least one mentioned the platform isn’t a revenue source. “I don’t make a penny because Instagram doesn’t pay out for anything, in terms of Reels. So I make zero money on Instagram, but I had 450 million views last month,” said Parnas, who added he gets much higher viewership on the platform than elsewhere, but frustratingly isn’t earning anything for that elevated engagement.

Newly independent news creator Mark Istook, said he found Instagram to be easier to predict, and the comments are far less “poisonous and toxic” than on sibling Meta-owned platform, Facebook.

The jury is split on Instagram’s utility. While creator Grant Hermes found Instagram might as well be a “black box” in terms of understanding its underlying structure, creator Adam Mockler, however, has figured it out, boasting 90 million views a month on the platform.

Mockler pulls in even more views on Facebook, boasting 120 million on the platform which he called an “underdog” that has been “really swinging lately.”

“I genuinely think Zuckerberg changed something when Trump got into office,” he said. “They opened politics back up on Facebook, and it allowed advertisers to buy up spots on political stuff again.” It’s performing so well, Mockler has doubled down, putting more resources and more team members, he did not offer specifics, on the platform, even hiring an intern that helps post on the platform.

Parnas said he also focuses on Facebook because it, along with YouTue, is where the most revenue comes from.

Mockler also told Digiday he’s leaning into Reddit, a platform notoriously difficult to break through.

“We had a 17-year-old high schooler posting on Reddit for a while…I view Reddit as more valuable than TIkTok right now,” Mockler said, adding about 70 percent of the intern’s role was him posting his appearances on CNN or other traditional news platforms in several subreddits, while the rest was on the official Mockler Media Reddit account, all to get people to engage with his content and find him on other platforms. “I view it as internal PR,” Mockler said.

Spehar and others said TikTok has been a tough platform lately, with inconsistent flagging and engagement hampering their revenue and reach.

“[TikTok] blocks political content or mislabels it as ads and live streams focusing on politics can be falsely reported. Overall it’s hard to get your perspective out if you don’t already have a big channel at this point,” Spehar said, adding the platform is still a good place for creators to build, but is more saturated than it once was.

Parnas placed TikTok on the third rung of his monetization scheme, underneath YouTube and Facebook and alongside Instagram and Istook said the platform should be more reliable when it comes to engagement, but is “all over the map” after it closed a deal with the U.S. earlier this year.

“My views on TikTok have remained very, very consistent over the past year,” said Parnas. Yet just a few days ago, one of his political videos got stuck in an “under review” stage for nearly three hours. “I just took it down,” he said.

Istook said he has the biggest audience on TIkTok but found the platform the least predictable in terms of engagement. “Something will do millions of views, and something else will do 1,500, which just is hard to make sense of with a follower count like I have,” he said.

#7348. ‘They hold us hostage’: Publishers furious over terms change still can’t quit Facebook

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Publisher trade groups around the world are pressing Meta to change its planned terms of service, but heads of audience and social media told Digiday that their continued reliance on its platforms raises questions about how much leverage they have to force changes.

The tension is familiar: publishers want to stop tech platforms from using their journalism in AI tools without compensation, but can’t easily give up the reach those platforms provide.

Meta recently announced it would consult with industry groups and leaders before updating its terms, but that has done little to pacify publishers. Several publisher trade groups Digiday spoke with, including the News Media Alliance, Digital Content Next, and The Professional Publishers Association, said they hadn’t yet been in contact with them.

In a video posted to LinkedIn, Tami Hoffman, director of public policy at The Guardian, warned that some publishers may ultimately decide to abandon Meta’s platforms over the company’s updated terms of use.

“Policy moves slowly, and some publishers may take the tough decision to come off these platforms altogether. This issue is filling up my inbox,” Hoffman said in the video.

That possibility is already weighing on publishers as they watch to see if Meta makes changes to its terms or provides detail on how publishers can opt out, according to Melissa Chowning, founder and CEO of Twenty-First Digital, an audience development and marketing consultancy for publishers and brands.

“The ones paying close attention are still asking questions about whether Meta traffic is worth the trade-off. Now they just have more time to make that call. I suspect if the terms don’t change, we’ll see several publishers move off of Meta. Hard to say how many that would be,” she said.

But internally, the situation is more complicated.

And that’s because Meta’s value to publishers is not really its referral traffic anymore. Heads of audience at three publishers told Digiday Meta’s social platforms are still crucial for other audience development and business strategies: branded content, paid media, reach, engagement, awareness, and connecting with community. They aren’t ready to cut ties, putting into question how much leverage publishers really have in their fight against Meta’s AI scraping terms.

One head of audience said the publisher had deprioritized Facebook after traffic from Meta’s platforms fell from accounting for more than a fifth of its global traffic to a negligible share. But Instagram remains an important platform for brand awareness, community engagement and marketing rather than referral traffic, they said.

“Meta Zero is already here,” the head of audience said.

When analyzing social traffic to the top 10 publisher websites (including The New York Post, The Wall Street Journal, The New York Times, People, BBC, AP News, CNN, USA Today, Fox News and CNBC), Facebook traffic was down 5% year-over-year, according to Similarweb data shared with Digiday.

Meta’s platforms accounted for an average of 3.47% of global publisher traffic in July 2026, according to Chatbeat’s most recent data, which includes over 2,500 domains.

The head of audience said they didn’t think it was likely they would remove themselves from Meta’s platforms.

“They hold us hostage,” they added. “This change in terms is another brazen move by Meta.”

It echoes the debate over blocking Google’s crawlers. Publishers have threatened to do so over AI scraping, but have yet to follow through, partly because they still rely on Google for search discovery and traffic, even as referrals decline.

Publishers’ relationships with Google and Meta have some important parallels, but they also differ in significant ways, stressed Eildah Wilson, head of policy and public affairs at the U.K. publisher trade body the PPA. “Both platforms wield enormous power over audience access, making withdrawal a ‘nuclear option’ for publishers,” she said.

The key difference is how publishers use these platforms, she explained. “Referral traffic from Meta has declined sharply, particularly since 2023, when it deprioritized branded content,” said Wilson. The result: publishers that had invested millions in social media teams and content saw traffic collapse almost overnight.

However, declining traffic has not diminished publishers’ reliance on Meta or its market power, stressed Wilson. “Publishers have adapted, using Meta for audience engagement, conversion and subscription acquisition,” she said. “A publisher may receive relatively little referral traffic from Meta but still depend on it for a significant proportion of new subscriptions. Referral traffic alone therefore understates Meta’s power over publishers. This is why we need an SMS [Strategic Market Status] investigation into Meta, as we’ve seen with Google Search.”

Another head of audience at a news publisher said social platforms remain critical to reaching new audiences, building awareness around its journalism and connecting with readers on platforms where they spend their time.

“I don’t see us abandoning our social accounts,” they said. “I think publishers can recognize the downsides of these relationships while still seeing tremendous value in being on these platforms. It’s not an either-or proposition.”

A third head of audience wasn’t yet certain how their outlet would evaluate its relationship with Meta going forward, as it was a decision that would be made at a more senior executive level. But they had strong words about the upcoming terms change.

“This is another example of Meta using brute force to get what it wants from the publishing industry. Publishers are being asked to accept sweeping access to their sites as the price of reaching audiences on Meta’s platforms. It’s a shameless pursuit of Meta’s own benefit, with little regard for the sustainability of the journalism its products depend on,” they said.

Meta has also recently started charging brands for link posts, which can help drive audiences to their sites. While publishers are excluded from this, the first head of audience wondered for how long.

All of this points to why a number of publishing trade bodies are turning to regulators and lawmakers to pressure Meta into changing its terms, leading to developments like the CMA investigation, the Norwegian court injunction and U.S. Senators urging Meta to abandon the proposed terms.

Wilson said Meta needs to “unbundle” its terms and separate access to its platform from permission to crawl publishers’ content. Publishers should be able to allow link previews or advertising while blocking uses such as AI training and product development, explained Wilson. Those choices need to be enforceable: protections such as robots.txt should be written into binding terms, not left to public assurances, she added.

Publishers should also be told which crawlers and third parties collect their content, what they collect and how that material is used. Meta’s terms, Wilson argues, should not retroactively authorize past copying or make publishers responsible for content they do not have the rights to license.

“This goes beyond communication and transparency. The terms themselves must change,” said Wilson. “As drafted, they leave publishers with an impossible choice: withdraw from services their businesses depend on, or accept crawling and use of their content without firm legal safeguards. Regardless of Meta’s intentions, that legal exposure is unacceptable.”

Political and news creators are relied on now more than ever, but what platforms are working for them? Which ones aren’t?

#7346. Senior Infrastructure Engineer II at Kit

Kit is the email marketing platform experts choose to grow their audience, deepen their relationships, and build a business around the work they love. For coaches, YouTubers, authors, podcasters, and other creatives, there isn't a better marketing hub to rely on to grow audiences, automate email marketing, and sell digital products — all within one platform.More importantly, there isn't a team more committed to helping creators earn a living. We're on a mission to help creators earn $1 billion using our creator marketing platform. We have always been 100% independent and 100% remote.We are proud to have built a product that our customers love, and we look for people who have enthusiasm and belief in our mission, vision, and values to join our team. We're also embracing AI thoughtfully — both in how we build and how we hire to ensure our team is adaptable, innovative, and ready for what's next.

We're looking for a Senior Infrastructure Engineer II to take ownership of a large share of Kit's core infrastructure: databases, Kubernetes, version upgrades, platform tooling, and day-to-day support. You'll also build the paved paths that let engineers, non-engineers, and AI agents test and ship changes safely.

The infrastructure supports creators who depend on Kit to reach their audiences and earn a living. When it's healthy, nobody notices. That's the job.

This is a full-time Individual Contributor role for an experienced infrastructure engineer who likes owning production systems end to end, takes on-call seriously, leans into automation and enjoys making other people's work easier.

You'll report to Mojtaba Hosseini, VP of Engineering at Kit. Day to day, you'll work most closely with Kris Hamoud and Ivan Mora Perez.

You'll also partner with product engineers across Kit, and with teams outside engineering who rely on infrastructure to get their work done. Once you're ramped up, you'll join a three-person on-call rotation, which works out to roughly one week in three.

First Week: Get access to our AWS accounts, Kubernetes clusters, monitoring, and CI/CD pipelines. Read through our runbooks and architecture docs. Take part in Get To Know Yous (GTKYs) with Kris, Ivan, and the engineering teams you'll support.

First Month: Pick up day-to-day infrastructure support requests and start working through them with Kris and Ivan as backup. Ship your first improvement, such as a version upgrade or a fix to a gap in monitoring. Shadow on-call shifts. Write down what you learn along the way, especially the parts that only lived in someone's head or those that can be automated or improved.

First Six Months: Own core infrastructure areas end to end: databases, Kubernetes, version upgrades, and platform tooling. Take your full share of on-call, which you'll start by the end of your first quarter. Become the go-to person for day-to-day infrastructure questions. Find one recurring source of pages and make it go away. Ship a first paved path, such as a sandbox environment where AI agents or non-engineers can safely build and test changes.

First Year: You're the primary owner of a large part of Kit's core infrastructure, and you lead projects in it without needing direction. Uptime stays at 99.9% or better, page volume is down, and you've found real cost savings. Your paved paths are in regular use by engineers, non-engineers, and AI agents. You've built observability good enough for AI agents to handle some on-call load. You've documented your decisions so others can build on your work.

Running production infrastructure on AWS at scale, including managed relational databases, networking, and load balancing

Operating Kubernetes in production: upgrades, capacity planning, and troubleshooting

Managing infrastructure as code and maintaining CI/CD pipelines that engineers trust

Building monitoring and alerting that catches real problems early and doesn't page people for noise

Diagnosing complex, multi-service incidents calmly, then fixing the underlying cause

Cutting cloud costs through right-sizing and reserved capacity without hurting reliability

Writing clear runbooks, docs, and decision write-ups

Nice to have: experience with Cloudflare, ephemeral or sandbox environments, or email sending infrastructure

Around 7 or more years in infrastructure, platform, DevOps, or SRE roles

Owning production infrastructure for a product where downtime costs customers money

Carrying a pager for systems you owned, and making that on-call rotation quieter over time

Leading a major upgrade or migration, such as a database or Kubernetes version upgrade, with little or no customer impact

Building self-service tooling or paved paths that other engineers actually adopted

Working on a small infrastructure team where you couldn't hand problems off to a specialist

You own what you run. You spot infrastructure debt and fix it without waiting to be asked, and you follow through until it's done.

You build quality into the work. Upgrades have rollback plans, maintenance windows are planned around customers, and "good enough" is a decision, not a default.

You leverage AI effectively. AI helps you increase quality and velocity where it matters most. You know when to delegate and when to dive in, while holding accountability.

You make work easier for everyone else. You'd rather build a tool or write a guide once than answer the same question ten times.

You teach what you know. You write things down so the next person, or the next AI agent, can build on your work.

You work in public. You share context and tradeoffs openly, including during incidents and when things go wrong.

You use AI thoughtfully. It's part of how you work, and you help others adopt it without creating new risks.

You remember who's on the other end. An outage means a creator's launch email doesn't go out, and that shapes how you prioritize.

Wanting a well-defined backlog handed to you each week. Here, you'll find much of the work yourself and set priorities with Kris.

Preferring not to be on call. On-call is a core part of this role, shared across a team of three.

You are skeptical of AI’s capabilities, or conversely use AI without good judgement and accountability.

Wanting mostly greenfield architecture. A lot of this work is running, upgrading, and improving systems that already exist.

Needing layers of approval before acting.

We're 100% remote with team members across the globe. We work mostly on East Coast time—so if you're based on the West Coast of the United States, Europe, or Asia, you might have a few early mornings or late evenings. We do our best to keep things reasonable, but a little time zone juggling comes with the territory.

This role is part of a three-person on-call rotation, so during your on-call week you'll need to be able to respond to pages outside your usual working hours.

We believe PTO should always take priority over on-call. We schedule on-call around people's lives, not the other way around. On-call is a shared responsibility, but taking time off should never be a challenge

#7344. Can ad tech’s server-side shift save it from Apple and Google?

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Apple has reminded ad tech how much power the browser holds. Ad tech’s reaction: Get out of the browser.

With iOS 27, Apple’s Safari browser has begun blocking domains tied to identity resolution providers, such as The Trade Desk and ID5, that link people’s browsing to their email addresses. In response, ad tech firms and IAB Tech Lab are pushing to move ad calls server-side, including through the latter organization’s Trusted Server technology, which would limit the browser’s control over ad tech firms’ actions.

Meanwhile, the remedies ruling in Google’s ad tech antitrust trial has given Prebid an opening to flip the programmatic auction. Instead of Google’s ad server getting final say on the winning bid, Google’s ad exchange would have to bid into Prebid’s server.

But is the server-side shift a long-term solution? And is it the right one? Digiday’s executive editor of news Seb Joseph and senior ad tech reporter Ronan Shields joined the Digiday Podcast to hash out ad tech’s Game of Thrones amid the server-side shift.

Joseph: From the conversations we’re having in the initial aftermath of the Safari crackdown, Trusted Server has been seen as almost a panacea. And yet it does feel like that is a misappropriation of what Trusted Server was designed for, and that any attempt to truly lean on it as a durable workaround to what Apple is doing misses the point entirely. It’s pretty clear that Apple is not going to stop at the companies that it has already targeted and, more importantly, the mechanism that they use to do that. Whether it’s happening via Safari or via Trusted Server, Apple does not want that stuff happening to its customers in any guise and will by hook or crook find a way to offset that.

Shields: Can ad tech wrest back control from these power brokers of Apple and Google? I would say absolutely no. They can’t wrest control because Google and Apple own the browser, the mobile OS — in the case of Google — they own the ad tech platform and the cloud infrastructure as well. With Trusted Server, [ad tech companies] can gain a degree of autonomy, but they need to behave themselves with it to retain that autonomy.

Joseph: Is it incumbent on the industry at some point to get its shit together and do more than the bare minimum, the baseline set by regulators, in order to instill some sort of trust? Because otherwise the browsers, the platforms, they will find a way to put their hand on the scale moving forward.

Shields: I hear what you’re saying, Seb, but how realistic is that?

Joseph: I’d say it was pretty realistic this time around, because otherwise what happens?

Shields: If we listen to [Google’s] earnings yesterday [Oct. 28], the one aspect of their business that didn’t do well again is this [third-party display ad] network business, the GAM area of the business. I think they’re OK with letting it go. So Prebid wants it? Yeah, fine, go ahead. I don’t think they’re going to lose too much sleep over it.

Google’s Q3 earnings reveal AI Mode’s impact on search advertising growth, plus ongoing diminishing returns from ad tech.

#7339. Agency execs call for retail media to be holistically considered beyond the lower funnel

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

As it continues to expand in the number of channels and options, as well as the ad revenue placed into it, retail media is offering media agencies new opportunities to harness closed-loop measurement in a way that benefits both upper- and lower-funnel marketing efforts.

That’s one major takeaway from a conversation onstage Dentsu’s president of commerce, North America, Aisha Khan, at Digiday’s Media Buying Summit last week in Newport Beach, Calif., last week. Khan said she also believes that the in-store side of retail media has great upward potential.

“There’s still tremendous growth to be had in the commerce landscape,” Khan told the audience, citing the stat that more than 80% of sales are still happening in store, but less than 5% of retail media is spent on in-store media. “So if you think about that expansion opportunity for retailers to really digitize the physical experience, that is going to create tremendous tremendous growth for retail media — think about that last point of purchase.”

In store may also be a way to bring down the expense of reaching consumers at the lower-end of the purchase funnel. “Retail media is very effective, right? It’s at one of the lowest points of in the funnel, and but it’s very expensive, so the efficiency is not quite there,” said Kahn. “So I think in a good way there’ll be a bit more balance between the effectiveness and the efficiency, which will help to actually grow the next phase of commerce media, so I think while some areas will be will come down or plateau, there will be other areas of growth like the in-store piece.”

And in an earlier session on Day 2 of the Media Buying Summit, Instrument CEO Laurel Burton talked about how all the distraction around AEO and GEO has made the industry overlook the value of websites as part of an overall commerce strategy when, to her mind, the opposite is true.

“My prediction is the exact opposite that websites are dying,” Burton told the audience at a session on the melding of creative and tech with media. “I think they are the absolute central hub that needs to create a consistent experience because they’re feeding both that consumer and also that lanswer engine. You literally can’t be a product and a brand without this as a channel.”

Burton’s fellow speaker, Crocs CMO Carly Gomez (who happens to be an Instrument client), elaborated on Burton’s position saying the website needs to be coordinated in its messaging with other marketing channels.

“As people are discovering your brand, yes, they may come in through an LLM,” Gomez said. “But then if they want more information on your brand, their next stop either is your social media site or one of your social media handles or your e-commerce or your app. And essentially you want them all to feel cohesive and deliver the best possible experience.”

And yet, the upper-funnel brand-building side of retail media can’t be overlooked, argued Dentsu’s Khan, who pointed to the likes of CVS and Kroger as two retail media network providers who are helping to push the creative boundaries further (but didn’t elaborate).

“It goes back to ensuring that you’re getting the incrementality and the efficiency out of it, and that it’s worth the extra dollars that you’re paying for the closed-loop measurement,” she said. “People forget that there’s so much opportunity to come back to the power of creative in retail media, and I think retail media has been relegated to this performance marketing channel. But if you think about it, with the in-store and the shopper marketing experience, you still need that unmissable creative component too.”

Having just also picked up Diageo’s shopper marketing business, Khan noted that AI-powered Media Mix Modeling (MMM) will also help bring down the cost, even as consolidation will likely reduce the total number of retail media networks out there.

“Retail media’s boom was based on the back of closed loop measurement — this promise that you could understand with every dollar what you’re actually driving on the other side,” said Khan. “But I also believe AI has accelerated this idea of of agile MMM, or incrementality — more traditional MMM becoming more sophisticated. And so, as you think about closed-loop measurement, advertisers are investing in their own capabilities to invest across retailers and get that closed loop measurement themselves, instead of having the retailers grade their own homework. What happens is that data CPM that retailers have been charging for closed-loop measurement potentially does compress.”

#7336. Ad Tech Briefing: Google’s AI-reckoning

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This Ad Tech Briefing covers the latest in ad tech and platforms for Digiday+ members and is distributed over email every Tuesday at 10 a.m. ET. More from the series →

It’s earnings season, typically the time of year when industry observers watch agog, as Big Tech companies post earnings far greater than the average ad tech company can begin to contemplate, but Q3 this year has thrown us a curveball to the usual narrative.

The unexpected glitch in the latest round ran contrary to the expected narrative, i.e., Google’s punishment for the success of its AI, a testament to the extent of the harsh realities facing the digital media sector as a whole when it comes to sustaining revenue.

It’s also likely to serve as an important footnote in the future filings of Google’s legal department, with tuned-in observers likely to recall how it was able to cite rapid AI advancements as proof of a fiercely competitive and evolving tech landscape.

Even if Google did lose both trials (on paper, at least) last year, it can be argued the above argument was made successfully to justify it continuing without forced divestiture, and in Q3 (it would appear) this argument impacted the biggest company in advertising’s bottom line.

Since then, Google has spent much of the year telling advertisers and investors that AI will make its search business bigger, not smaller. However, last week’s earnings offered the clearest indication yet that delivering on that promise is proving complicated.

On Oct. 28, Google’s parent company Alphabet reported that its search queries were growing at their fastest annual rate in five years, with total Q3 revenues nearing $127.6 billion, up 25% year-on-year.

However, a deeper analysis reveals a more troubling trend for the hegemon of Madison Avenue: more people are searching, but Google’s AI-driven search experiences aren’t yet generating ad revenue at the same rate as conventional search.

However, revenue from Google’s “Search & other” operations increased 12% to $63.35 billion, with Google’s chief business officer Philipp Schindler acknowledging as much during the company’s third-quarter earnings call, telling analysts that AI Mode’s comparatively limited monetization had contributed to the slowdown.

The disclosure is significant because search remains Google’s biggest financial engine and supports its (increasingly expensive) AI ambitions.

Advertising revenue, i.e., search in combination with other areas of its media operations, such as “YouTube” (Q3 revenues of $11.2 billion) and “Network” (Q3 revenues of $7.45 billion) — that’s the ad tech side, which is continuing its prolonged decline in revenue — accounts for approximately 64% of Alphabet’s revenue, down from 72% a year earlier.

More queries, less revenue per opportunity

Wall Street was unimpressed. Alphabet shares fell approximately 12.6% on Oct. 29, erasing close to $500 billion in market value, as investors questioned the economics of the company’s AI transition with coverage in the immediate aftermath of the results highlighting disappointment in Google’s revenue performance.

Google maintains that AI is expanding search activity rather than merely replacing existing queries. Sundar Pichai, CEO, Alphabet, told analysts that AI Mode queries are, on average, three times longer than traditional searches, while approximately one in five involves voice, images or documents.

However, executives also acknowledged that AI Mode currently monetizes at a lower rate, and further hinted that it is now expanding advertising within AI Mode internationally and introducing multi-turn advertisements in the U.S., allowing commercial placements to appear during extended conversations.

Meanwhile, the continued struggles of “Google Network” revenues, i.e., ad tech, is likely to stimulate further grimacing from publishers, with this unit generating a modest 1.4% gain in revenues during the period, with this trend accompanied by an overall increase in traffic acquisition costs, or “TAC,” of approximately 10% during the period.

Overall, TAC hit $16.4 billion, with the figures highlighting how higher impression prices can mask shrinking volumes as publishers contend with declining search referrals and unpredictable traffic. This creates an awkward backdrop for Google’s court-ordered ad tech remedies. Prebid’s proposed auction overhaul could improve publisher control and interoperability, but neither can restore lost audiences or guarantee advertiser demand.

“Can we really blame Apple for blocking ID tech?”

#7313. Kay Sends Colin Jost on a Gift-Seeking Odyssey for the Holiday Season

Kay recruited Saturday Night Live star Colin Jost for a holiday campaign that jokes about the pressure of finding the right gift.

In the ad, the comedian embarks on a treacherous journey, inspired by Homer’s The Odyssey, in search of a thoughtful present.

Amid a blizzard, a bearded Jost in a period-appropriate robe finally stumbles upon the gift he had been hunting for: a silver Kay necklace.

The campaign, created by The Martin Agency, departs from Kay’s previous holiday work, which focused primarily on the person receiving a gift. This ad instead addresses the annual struggle gift givers face when deciding what to buy.

According to the American Psychological Association’s 2023 Holiday Stress Survey, 40% of people have trouble finding the right gifts, making it one of the biggest stressors of the season. Kay wants to encourage people to “gift boldly,” with the message that “the biggest mistake isn’t gifting the wrong thing; it’s holding back,” per the brand.

“The holidays often bring pressure to find the perfect gift, but we want to remind shoppers that the most meaningful gifts come from expressing how you feel and showing up wholeheartedly for the people you love,” said Julie Yoakum, president of Kay. “Colin’s signature humor brings that idea to life in a way that resonates with anyone who’s ever second-guessed what to gift someone they care about.”

The campaign builds on Kay’s Love All In platform, which launched earlier this fall and introduced a new brand tone meant to capture the many ways love shows up today. It revives the idea of grand gestures, such as taking an odyssey, at a time when nonchalance has become the norm, according to the brand.

The work begins running this week across Kay’s Instagram and YouTube channels, linear TV, and streaming.

Publicis has closed its $2.2 billion acquisition of data collaboration platform LiveRamp, the company said on Monday.

The ad network is touting the all-cash deal, first announced in May, as its solution to agentic transformation: using AI agents to automate and collaborate on corporate workflows, while giving clients full ownership of those systems.

The acquisition was finalized just over a week after the Federal Trade Commission cleared it, subject to five years of neutrality requirements for Publicis and LiveRamp.

Publicis will continue to operate LiveRamp as an independent company, with Scott Howe remaining as CEO.

#7288. Ahead of Midterm Elections, Murdoch Acknowledges Fox News Ratings Struggles

$4.57 billion — Fox Corp. had a 22% increase in total quarterly revenue from the prior year.

47% — Advertising revenue increase from the final stages of the FIFA World Cup 2026, political ad spend from the midterm elections, and Tubi’s continued growth.

$350 million — Political advertising spend across the business, a record for a midterm cycle.

This year’s summer period between July and September was a boon for Fox Corp., bookended by two major sporting events: the final rounds of the FIFA World Cup 2026 and the start of the 2026-’27 NFL Season.

The World Cup final between Spain and Argentina gave Fox Sports its most watched soccer telecast in U.S. history. During the company’s fiscal first-quarter earnings of 2027, Lachlan Murdoch, executive chair and CEO of Fox Corporation, said the World Cup, the start of MLB postseason, and the opening of the NFL and college football seasons “have boosted views and engagement” at the network.

When asked during the investor Q&A portion of the call about securing the rights to the next tournament in 2030 and 2034, Murdoch said they will approach it in a disciplined manner, as they do with all their other sports rights acquisitions.

Murdoch said political advertising has surpassed $350 million during this midterm election cycle, with most of that revenue coming from Fox’s owned-and-operated stations. Tubi also benefited from targeted ad spend as campaigns recognized the streaming platform’s “large, mostly cordless audience.”

Interestingly, Murdoch touted Fox News’ viewership strength compared to its rivals but acknowledged its recent ratings struggles, calling it difficult to compare to the previous year. He said the network is experiencing viewer fatigue, adding it’s a trend they have seen before, referencing a similar pattern during the 2006 midterm elections, but was confident about overcoming it.

Murdoch went on to say its viewers are not crossing over to MS NOW and CNN; they are just tuning out.

Murdoch did not provide additional details on its deal to purchase Roku, saying it is going through the appropriate regulatory process and hopes to close the transaction as soon as it can.

“As you would expect, audiences have turned to Fox for our comprehensive coverage of this election cycle, with national coverage led by the Fox News team while our broadcast stations provide essential local perspective, particularly in many battleground states. Heading into tomorrow’s vote, more Americans have relied on Fox News than any other news network for coverage of the key issues and news this cycle,” —Lachlan Murdoch, executive chair and CEO, Fox Corporation

The all-cash deal value comes out to $11.70 per share, a premium of 34.5% on Perion’s Oct. 30 closing price.

Affle has traditionally used AI to help advertisers improve targeting, engagement, and sales. The companies say the deal will help deliver a more unified, full-funnel approach, bringing together Affle’s lower-funnel mobile performance marketing business and Perion’s focus on attracting upper-funnel brand dollars across CTV, retail media, and DOOH.

Together, the capabilities could support “CTV-to-cart” and “DOOH-to-cart” outcomes for advertisers, Affle hopes.

The mashup, per a statement from Affle CEO, chair, and managing director Anuj Khanna Sohum, could “unlock greater ROI for advertisers and publishers alike, while driving global scale for premium consumer conversions across industry verticals such as e-commerce, retail, and healthcare.” It will also bolster Affle’s cost-per-converted-user mobile business in developed markets and support its goal of 10x-ing its growth in a decade, Sohum said.

With Perion’s capabilities folded in, Affle will now have “a way to both expand geographically to become global, and to garner upper-funnel capabilities in out-of-home, CTV, and commerce media,” said Terence Kawaja, founder and CEO of investment bank Luma Partners, which served as the sole financial advisor to Affle on the deal. “If you have an excellent conversion technology capability, then apply that to upper-funnel agency-led advertising solutions, you can basically turn those into outcomes,” he said.

The pairing will “create new opportunities for brands, agencies, and media owners worldwide,” Tal Jacobson, Perion CEO, added in a statement.

But the price tag was also relatively cheap considering it’s about a fraction of what Perion used to be worth, and Perion’s on-hand cash offsets about half the cost. Excluding the roughly $228 million in cash and investments on Perion’s balance sheet as of Sept. 30, Affle is effectively paying about $192 million for the operating business. That’s roughly 3.7x the $51 million to $53 million in adjusted EBITDA that Perion has projected for this year.

Neither Affle nor Perion responded to requests for comment on the deal value.

The agreement adds to a wider pattern of open-web consolidation following a succession of mergers and acquisitions this year: In May Publicis spent $2.5 billion on LiveRamp; Nielsen agreed to pay $2.15 billion to take DoubleVerify private in August; Stagwell’s acquired publisher adtech platform Freestar last week; Private equity firms Vista Equity Partners and Quinti Capital are also reportedly making a bid for Criteo.

Budgets keep moving to walled gardens, retail media, and CTV. Recent findings from the World Advertising Research Center indicate that Alphabet, Amazon, and Meta will capture almost 60% of global ad spend outside China this year. Perion historically bought into those channels, but faces intense competition in those arenas from bigger players like Amazon, which gobbled up over 75% of U.S. retail media spend last year, per Emarketer.

Perion’s own CEO said in August that the company was built around “diversifying away from the open web,” and its web channel was the primary drag on revenue in the second quarter. Historically dependent on the open web and search syndication (thanks to a long-standing partnership with Microsoft Bing), and punished by public markets in recent years—even after a nearly $193 million cumulative stock repurchasing push through Sept. 30—Perion matches the profile for the kind of culling playing out across the market. Before the Affle deal was announced, Perion’s stock was down about 79% from its five-year high of nearly $42 in April 2023.

That’s not to say Perion didn’t try to spread its eggs across different baskets first. The company made a handful of acquisitions in recent years, including video adtech platform Vidazoo in 2021 ($93.5 million), DOOH player Hivestack in 2023 ($100 million), AI-powered media buying firm Greenbids in 2025 (up to $65 million), and Premier Retail Networks in August of this year (up to $12 million).

Luma Partners’ Kawaja acknowledged that the low price was “icing on the cake” for selling Perion on the deal. Affle, he said, “looked at a number of different potential targets, and they’re all trading fairly cheaply.” So the pricing, he said, “was just opportunistic.”

But he insisted the Perion takeover is more strategic than financial in nature, and said the pricing “didn’t drive” the decision. “These are not financial guys that said, ‘Hey, look, it’s trading low. We buy it.’ It’s a strategic rationale for the expansion [of the business] that fits the industry trends of moving towards outcomes and a collapse of the purchase funnel,” he said. “It just makes it that much easier and better if you don’t have to bet the ranch and pay a huge multiple.”

Under the terms of the arrangement, expected to close next year, Perion, which has traded on the Nasdaq since 2006, will become a subsidiary of Affle’s Israeli operation Affle 3iL Ltd, and will come off the exchange and the Tel Aviv exchange, where it also traded. The combined company will still trade on the public National Stock Exchange of India.

The acquisition is backed by debt financing from Citibank, and will require shareholder and U.S. antitrust approval. Terms also include a 40-day ‘go-shop’ period during which Perion can solicit rival offers.

Perion shares were up 31% Monday, to around $11.42, following the announcement.

Kendra Barnett is Adweek's senior tech reporter.

#7274. Mark Green, Droga5 global CEO and The Monkeys Co-Founder, Dies at 53

Mark Green, global CEO of Droga5 and co-founder of Australian agency The Monkeys, died last Saturday in New York City of a “sudden and unexpected” heart attack, according to a company statement. He was 53.

“Mark made an indelible mark on the advertising industry both in Australia and globally,” the statement read. “He will be remembered not only as the visionary that he was, but for his generosity, his humor, and his empathy.”

Green—nicknamed “Greenie” by friends—was appointed global CEO of Droga5 in 2024, relocating from Australia to New York City with his family for the position. He told ADWEEK he was brought on to globalize Droga5 and prove the agency’s “glory days” were not in the rearview:

“Truly, this is the first time Droga5 has been global,” Green said at the time.

Green co-founded The Monkeys in 2006 alongside Justin Drape and Scott Nowell, whom he met at Saatchi & Saatchi. What began as an idea hatched among three friends at an Australian café became the country’s most-awarded agency for its work with The Sydney Opera House, Telstra, Qantas, Ikea, Airbnb, and Intel.

In 2017, Accenture acquired The Monkeys for a reported $63 million, later merging it with Droga5. Green oversaw the integration as Accenture Song’s president of Australia and New Zealand from 2019 to 2024.

Always quick to champion the work of his teams, Green took the spotlight himself in 2025 as an inductee into the AdNews Australia’s Advertising Hall of Fame:

“Someone once said to me that to make it in the advertising industry, you need to be smart enough to play the game, but dumb enough to think that it matters,” he wrote. “And here we are.”

Green is survived by his wife Rachel and his two children, Ari and Gisele. Droga5’s leadership said there is “more to come” on how his legacy will be celebrated in the coming weeks.

Many current and former colleagues took to LinkedIn this morning to offer condolences and give tribute to “Greenie’s” legacy:

“Everyone who knew Greenie is a better person for having had him in their lives,” wrote Grant Patrick Flannery, who worked with Green at The Monkeys and Droga5.

#7261. Security Engineer, Infrastructure Security at LiveKit

LiveKit is building the infrastructure layer for the voice-driven era of computing. Our platform gives developers everything they need to build, test, deploy, scale, and observe agents in production. Founded in 2021, LiveKit powers voice AI applications for OpenAI, xAI, Salesforce, Coursera, Spotify, and thousands of others, collectively facilitating billions of calls each year.

Our customers trust us with audio, video and data belonging to their own end users, so a security failure on our side puts their data and their business at risk. We are hiring an Infrastructure Security Engineer to improve the security posture of the production platform that carries that data. You will join a small Trust team that covers security, IT and compliance, and you will own real parts of the program from your first week. You will build our understanding of infrastructure security posture, then prioritize and drive remediation by business impact. Where the same class of problem keeps coming back, you will build the guardrails and security building blocks that let our security scale with the business.

think like both a builder and a breaker, and understand security from first principles

take an automation-first and pragmatic approach to risk mitigation

translate security concerns into engineering action

can clearly explain complex technical concepts to others

are a fast learner and thrive in ambiguity

Map the security posture of our cloud and production infrastructure and turn it into a documented baseline.

Find the gaps that could lead to customer data loss or infrastructure compromise, agree a prioritized security debt backlog with engineering, and close the highest-risk items first.

Harden identity, secrets and network boundaries, including cloud IAM, environment separation and credential hygiene.

#7260. Darshan Kantak Joins Netflix as VP of Ads Platform

Darshan Kantak is joining Netflix as vice president of its ads platform.

In this new role, Kantak will lead the teams building the advertising experience on the service and enable reporting and operational tools for Netflix Ads Suite partners.

“What drew me to Netflix is the chance to use that technology to build impactful solutions,” Kantak said. These include “ads that feel relevant and welcome for members, and technical tools that make advertising even more innovative and effective. I look forward to building that with the team.”

He was previously VP of product at Google Cloud’s Applied AI, and has also worked at Snap Inc., Meta, Microsoft, and Yahoo.

Reporting to Amy Reinhard, president of advertising, Kantak will have oversight of the advertising product, engineering, and data & insights teams developing strategic ad platform initiatives for Netflix around the world.

“Darshan’s expertise spans from innovative technical skills to unique AI tooling and operations, all of which I’m excited for him to bring to the Netflix ads business,” said Reinhard.

His appointment comes at an interesting time for the streamer, as its in-house ad platform last month announced updates that included new measurement capabilities and an expanded programmatic offering.

These new Netflix Ads Suite updates come as the streamer was expecting to double its advertising revenue for the second consecutive year. But during its Q3 earnings call last month, Netflix said it was lowering that amount to $2.7 billion because of a “slightly slower than expected rollout of some ad product capabilities.”

Amid all that, Netflix has recently undergone major personnel changes, including in its leadership ranks, such as the departure of chief content officer Bela Bajaria, who was replaced by Peter Rice, a former Disney and Fox exec. The media company also laid off about 6% of its staff, or about 1,000 employees, earlier this month.

Other major names who recently departed Netflix include film boss Dan Lin and TV executives Lisa Lang McMullen, James Oh, Carolina Garcia, and Andrew McQuinn. Doug Belgrad, Netflix’s film VP, also exited the company.

#7253. Senior ML Engineer - Malware Detection (remote work) at CloudLinux

CloudLinux is a global remote-first company. We are driven by our principles: do the right thing, employees first, we are remote first, and we deliver high-volume, low-cost Linux infrastructure and security products that help companies to increase the efficiency of their operations. Every person on our team supports each other and does what we can to ensure we all are successful.

Check out our website for more information: https://cloudlinux.com/

Imunify360 is CloudLinux's security suite for Linux web servers: real-time and background malware scanning, automated cleanup, a WAF, runtime PHP protection (Proactive Defense), a database malware scanner, and IP reputation. It runs on hosting providers' shared, VPS, and dedicated servers, so new web-malware campaigns land on our fleet early.

We sit on one of the largest labeled corpora of live web-server malware in the industry: files, verdicts, cleanup outcomes, and analyst decisions collected from production hosting servers every day. It already drives our signatures, heuristics, and ML models, and there is far more to get out of it. We are looking for a Senior ML Engineer who will turn that data into detection that ships — classifiers, LLM-assisted triage and generated signatures — and keep exploring new ML/LLM methods against it, measured by the product's false-negative and false-positive rates, not by notebook accuracy.

Our dataset focuses on web malware — PHP, JavaScript, HTML phishing kits, infected WordPress and Joomla plugins and themes, database injections — with the context around each file.

The goal is 10x more malware detected at the same or lower false-positive rate. Hand-written rules and faster pipelines will not get there; models trained on our data might.

You get an experimentation track, GPUs, and a direct line to leadership; in return, you bring numbers, not opinions.

You own the path from raw samples to a model in production, and the numbers that prove it helped.

Take a portfolio-scope growth surface — and own it from first impression to revenue.
